### SECT. XI.
<image-desc>decorative flourish scroll</image-desc>

<margin>Of the love of fame.</margin>
**All** this appears very probable in theory; but in order to provide full certainty to this reasoning, we must examine the phenomena of the passions and see if they agree with it.

**Among** these phenomena, we may consider it very favorable to our present purpose that, although fame in general is agreeable, we receive a much greater satisfaction from the approval of those whom we ourselves esteem and approve of than from those whom we hate and despise. In the same way, we are primarily mortified by the contempt of people whose judgment we value, and we are, to a great extent, indifferent to the opinions of the rest of mankind. But if the mind received from some original instinct a desire for fame and an aversion to infamy, then fame and infamy would influence us without distinction; and every opinion, depending on whether it was favorable or unfavorable, would equally excite that desire or aversion. The judgment of a fool is the judgment of another person just as much as that of a wise man, and it is only inferior in its influence on our own judgment.

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>A Treatise of Human Nature.</header>

### PART I.

<margin>
*Of pride and humility.*
</margin>

WE are not only better pleased with the approval of a wise man than with that of a fool, but receive an additional satisfaction from the former when it is obtained after a long and intimate acquaintance. This is accounted for in the same manner.

THE praise of others never gives us much pleasure unless it concurs with our own opinion and extols us for those qualities in which we chiefly excel. A mere soldier values the reputation of eloquence very little; a scholar <note>original: gownman</note>, courage; a bishop, humor; or a merchant, learning. Whatever esteem a man may have for any quality considered in the abstract, when he is conscious he does not possess it, the opinions of the whole world will give him little pleasure in that particular; and that is because they will never be able to sway his own opinion.

NOTHING is more common than for men of good families, but narrow circumstances, to leave their friends and country and seek their livelihood by humble and manual <note>original: mechanical</note> employments among strangers, rather than among those who are acquainted with their birth and education. "We shall be unknown where we go," they say. "Nobody will suspect from what family we are descended." We shall

<meta>catchword: shall</meta>

He also observes that individuals from high-status families often prefer anonymous, low-status work abroad to avoid the shame of being judged by those who know their background.</summary>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>

shall be removed from all our friends and acquaintances, and our poverty and meanness will by that means sit more easily upon us. In examining these sentiments, I find they afford many very convincing arguments for my present purpose.

First, we may infer from them that the uneasiness of being scorned <note>original: contemn'd</note> depends on sympathy, and that sympathy depends on the relation of objects to ourselves; since we are most uneasy under the contempt of persons who are both related to us by blood and near us in place <note>original: contiguous in place</note>. Hence we seek to diminish this sympathy and uneasiness by separating these relations, and placing ourselves in proximity <note>original: contiguity</note> to strangers and at a distance from relatives.

Secondly, we may conclude that relations are required for <term>sympathy</term>, not absolutely considered as relations, but by their influence in converting our ideas of the sentiments of others into the very sentiments themselves, by means of the association between the idea of their persons and that of our own. For here the relations of kinship <note>original: kindred</note> and proximity both exist; but not being united in the same persons, they contribute in a lesser degree to the sympathy.

<summary>Hume argues that the pain of being despised is a result of sympathy, which is intensified by both kinship and physical proximity. He explains that individuals avoid the judgment of family by moving far away, thereby weakening the psychological association that turns another person's contempt into one's own felt shame.</summary>

<header>A Treatise of Human Nature.</header>

<margin>
PART
I.
}
Of pride
and humi-
lity.

### T H I R D L Y,

This very circumstance—the lessening of sympathy by the separation of relations—is worthy of our attention. Suppose I am placed in poor circumstances among strangers and, consequently, am treated with little respect; I still find myself more at ease in that situation than when I was daily exposed to the contempt of my family <note>original: kindred</note> and fellow countrymen. Here I feel a double contempt: from my relatives, but they are absent; and from those around me, but they are strangers. This double contempt is likewise strengthened by the two relations of kinship <note>original: kindred</note> and proximity <note>original: contiguity</note>. But since the people who are connected to me by these two relations are not the same, this difference of ideas separates the impressions arising from the contempt and prevents them from blending into each other. The contempt of my neighbors has a certain influence, as does that of my family. However, these influences are distinct and never unite as they do when the contempt comes from people who are both my neighbors and my family. This phenomenon is similar to the system of pride and humility explained above, which may seem so extraordinary to the common understanding <note>original: vulgar apprehensions</note>.

<meta>catchword: F O U R T H L Y,</meta>

<summary>Hume explains that the psychological pain of being looked down upon is reduced when the sources of contempt—strangers and family—remain separate. Because the relations of kinship and proximity do not overlap in the same individuals, the negative feelings do not merge, making the experience more bearable.</summary>
<keywords>sympathy, contempt, kinship, proximity, pride, humility, social relations</keywords>

SECT. XII.
---
*Of the love of fame.*
</margin>

FOURTHLY, a person in these circumstances naturally conceals his birth from those among whom he lives, and is very uneasy if anyone suspects him to be of a family much superior to his present fortune and way of living. Everything in this world is judged by comparison. What is an immense fortune for a private gentleman is poverty for a prince. A peasant would think himself happy with what cannot provide the necessities for a gentleman. When a man has either been accustomed to a more splendid way of living, or thinks himself entitled to it by his birth and social status <note>original: quality</note>, everything below that level is disagreeable and even shameful; and it is with the greatest effort <note>original: industry</note> he conceals his claims to a better fortune. Here he himself knows his misfortunes; but as those with whom he lives are ignorant of them, he has the disagreeable reflection and comparison suggested only by his own thoughts, and never receives it through a sympathy with others; which must contribute very much to his ease and satisfaction.

*If there are any objections to this hypothesis—that the pleasure which we receive from praise arises from a communication of sentiments—we shall find, upon examination, that these objections, when taken in a proper*

Popular fame may be agreeable even to a man who despises the common people <note>original: the vulgar</note>; but it is because their numbers <note>original: multitude</note> give them additional weight and authority. Plagiarists <note>original: Plagiaries</note> are delighted with praises that they are conscious they do not deserve; but this is a kind of castle-building, where the imagination amuses itself with its own fictions and strives to render them firm and stable by a sympathy with the sentiments of others. Proud men are most shocked with contempt, though they do not most readily agree with it; but it is because of the opposition between the passion which is natural to them and that received by sympathy. An intense <note>original: violent</note> lover is likewise very much displeased when you blame and condemn his love; though it is evident your opposition can have no influence except by the hold it takes of himself and by his sympathy with you. If he despises you, or perceives you are joking <note>original: in jest</note>, whatever you say has no effect upon him.

Thus, in whatever light we consider this subject, we may still observe that the causes of pride and humility correspond exactly to our hypothesis, and that nothing can excite either of these passions unless it is both related to ourselves and produces a pleasure or pain independent of the passion. We have not only proved that a tendency to produce pleasure or pain is common to all the causes of pride or humility, but also that it is the only thing which is common; and consequently, it is the quality by which they operate. We have further proved that the most significant <note>original: considerable</note> causes of these passions are really nothing but the power of producing either agreeable or uneasy sensations; and therefore that all their effects—and among the rest, pride and humility—are derived solely from that origin. Such simple and natural principles, founded on such solid proofs, cannot fail to be received

### Part I.

<margin>
Of pride
and humi-
lity.
</margin>
It is usual for anatomists to join their observations and experiments on human bodies with those on animals, and from the agreement of these experiments to derive an additional argument for any particular hypothesis. It is indeed certain that where the structure of parts in animals is the same as in humans, and the operation of these parts is also the same, the causes of that operation cannot be different; and that whatever we discover to be true of the one species may be concluded without hesitation to be certain of the other. Thus, although the mixture of humors <note>bodily fluids</note> and the composition of minute parts may justly be presumed to be somewhat different in humans from what they are in mere animals—and therefore any experiment we make upon the one concerning the effects of medicines will not always apply to the other—yet as the structure of the veins and muscles, the fabric and situation of the heart, the lungs, the stomach, the liver, and other parts are the same or nearly the same in all animals, the very same hypothesis which in one species explains muscular motion, the progress of the chyle <note>milky fluid produced during digestion</note>, and the circulation of the blood, must be applicable to every

<meta>catchword: very</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that because the physical structures of humans and animals are so similar, a hypothesis explaining biological or psychological functions in one species should logically apply to the other.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, animals, human nature, physiology, species, hypothesis, pride, humility, humors, chyle</keywords>

every one; and according as it agrees or disagrees with the experiments we may make in any species of creatures, we may draw a proof of its truth or falsehood on the whole. Let us, therefore, apply this method of inquiry, which is found so just and useful in reasonings concerning the body, to our present anatomy of the mind, and see what discoveries we can make by it.

In order to this we must first show the correspondence of *passions* in men and animals, and afterwards compare the *causes* which produce these passions.

   It is plain that in almost every species of creatures, but especially of the nobler kind, there are many evident marks of pride and humility. The very port and gait of a swan, or turkey, or peacock show the high idea he has entertained of himself, and his contempt of all others. This is the more remarkable, in that in the two last species of animals, the pride always attends the beauty, and is discovered in the male only. The vanity and emulation of nightingales in singing have been commonly remarked; as likewise that of horses in swiftness, of hounds in sagacity and smell, of the bull and cock in strength, and of every other animal in

Add to this that every species of creature that interacts with humans often enough to become familiar with them shows an evident pride in human approval and is pleased by human praises and caresses, independent of any other consideration. Nor is it the caresses of just anyone without distinction that give them this vanity, but principally those of the people they know and love, in the same way that this passion is excited in humans. All these are clear proofs that pride and humility are not merely human passions but extend across the entire animal kingdom.

The *causes* of these passions are likewise much the same in beasts as in us, making a fair allowance for our superior knowledge and understanding. For example, animals have little or no sense of virtue or vice; they quickly lose track of blood relations and are incapable of understanding rights and property. For this reason, the causes of their pride and humility must lie solely in the body and can never be placed either in the mind or in external objects. ### SECT. XII.
---
Of the pride and humility of animals.
</margin>

THE next question is whether, since these passions are the same and arise from the same causes throughout the whole creation, the manner in which the causes operate is also the same. According to all rules of analogy, this is justly to be expected; and if we find upon trial that the explanation of these phenomena, which we use in one species, will not apply to the rest, we may presume that the explanation, however plausible, is in reality without foundation.

IN order to decide this question, let us consider that there is evidently the same *relation* of ideas, derived from the same causes, in the minds of animals as in those of humans. A dog that has hidden a bone often forgets the place; but when brought to it, his thought passes easily to what he formerly concealed by means of contiguity, which produces a relation among his ideas. In the same way, when he has been severely beaten in any place, he will tremble on his approach to it, even though he discovers no signs of any present danger. The effects of resemblance are not so remarkable; but as that

<summary>Hume proposes that the mechanisms causing pride and humility should be identical across species through analogy. He argues that animals possess a "relation of ideas" similar to humans, demonstrated by how a dog associates specific locations with hidden objects or past trauma.</summary>
<keywords>analogy, relation of ideas, contiguity, animal psychology, pride, humility, association of ideas</keywords>

<header>A Treatise of Human Nature.</header>

<margin>Of pride and humility.</margin>
**PART I.** relation makes a significant ingredient in causation, of which all animals show such evident judgment, we may conclude that the three relations of resemblance, contiguity, and causation operate in the same manner upon beasts as upon human creatures.

There are also instances of the relation of impressions sufficient to convince us that there is a union of certain affections with each other in the inferior species of creatures as well as in the superior, and that their minds are frequently conveyed through a series of connected emotions. A dog, when elevated with joy, naturally turns toward love and kindness, whether for his master or for the opposite sex. In the same way, when full of pain and sorrow, he becomes quarrelsome and ill-natured; and that passion, which at first was grief, is converted into anger by the slightest occasion.

Thus all the internal principles that are necessary in us to produce either pride or humility are common to all creatures; and since the causes that excite these passions are likewise the same, we may justly conclude that these causes operate in the same manner throughout the whole animal creation. My hypothesis is so simple, and

# P A R T II.

## *Of love and hatred.*

---

### S E C T. I.

#### *Of the object and causes of love and hatred.*

<margin>S E C T. I.</margin>
It is altogether impossible to give any definition of the passions of *love* and *hatred*; and that is because they produce merely a simple impression, without any mixture or composition. It would be just as unnecessary to attempt any description of them drawn from their nature, origin, causes, and objects; and that is both because these are the subjects of our present inquiry, and because these passions themselves are sufficiently known from our common feeling and experience. This we have already observed concerning pride and humility,

As the immediate *object* of pride and humility is self, or that identical person of whose thoughts, actions, and sensations we are intimately conscious; so the *object* of love and hatred is some other person, of whose thoughts, actions, and sensations we are not conscious. This is sufficiently evident from experience. Our love and hatred are always directed to some sensible being external to us; and when we talk of *self-love*, it is not in a proper sense, nor has the sensation it produces anything in common with that tender emotion which is excited by a friend or mistress. It is the same case with hatred. We may be mortified by our own faults and follies; but never feel any anger or hatred, except from the injuries of others.

But though the object of love and hatred is always some other person, it is plain that the object is not, properly speaking, the cause of these passions, or alone sufficient to excite

For since love and hatred are directly contrary in their sensation, and have the same object in common, if that object were also their cause, it would produce these opposite passions in an equal degree; and as they must, from the very first moment, destroy each other, none of them would ever be able to make its appearance. There must, therefore, be some cause different from the object.

If we consider the causes of love and hatred, we shall find they are very much diversified, and have not many things in common. The virtue, knowledge, wit, good sense, and good humor of any person produce love and esteem; as the opposite qualities produce hatred and contempt. The same passions arise from bodily accomplishments, such as beauty, force, swiftness, and dexterity; and from their contraries; as likewise from the external advantages and disadvantages of family, possessions, clothes, nation, and climate. There is not one of these objects, but what by its different qualities may produce love and esteem, or hatred and contempt.

From the view of these causes we may derive a new distinction between the *quality* that operates, and the *subject* on which it

**THERE** are few persons who are satisfied with their own character, or genius, or fortune, who are not also desirous of showing themselves to the world and of acquiring the love and approval of mankind. Now it is evident that the very same qualities and circumstances which are the causes of pride or self-esteem are also the causes of vanity or the desire for reputation; and that we always put to view those particulars with which we are best satisfied in ourselves. But if love and esteem were not produced by the same qualities as pride, according to whether these qualities are related to ourselves or others, this method of proceeding would be very absurd; nor could men expect a correspondence in the sentiments of every other person with those they have entertained themselves. It is true, few can form exact systems of the passions or reflect on their general nature and resemblances. But without such progress in philosophy, we are not subject to many mistakes in this particular, but are sufficiently guided by common experience, as well as by a kind of *presensation*; which tells us what will operate on others by what we feel immediately

Now observe with attention the nature of these passions, and their situation with respect to each other. It is evident that here are four affections, placed, as it were, in a square or regular connection with, and distance from, each other. The passions of pride and humility, as well as those of love and hatred, are connected together by the identity of their object, which for the first set of passions is the self, and for the second set is some other person. These two lines of communication or connection form two opposite sides of the square. Again, pride and love are agreeable passions; hatred and humility are uneasy ones. This similarity of sensation between pride and love, and that between humility and hatred, forms a new connection, and may be considered as the other two sides of the square. On the whole, pride is connected with humility, and love with hatred, by their objects or ideas: pride is connected with love, and humility with hatred, by their sensations or impressions.

<vocab>pride, humility, love, hatred, passions, friendship, enmity, self, sensation, impressions</vocab>

<summary>Hume illustrates the relationship between pride, humility, love, and hatred by arranging them in a square, where they are linked either by having the same object (self or other) or by producing the same type of sensation (pleasure or pain).</summary>
<keywords>pride, humility, love, hatred, self, others, sensations, impressions, associations, psychological structure</keywords>

<margin>
SECTION II.
*Experiments to confirm this system.*
</margin>

I say, then, that nothing can produce any of these passions without bearing a double relation to it: namely, of ideas to the object of the passion, and of sensation to the passion itself. We must prove this through our experiments.

**FIRST EXPERIMENT.** To proceed with greater order in these experiments, let us first suppose that, being placed in the situation mentioned above—namely, in the company of some other person—an object is presented that has no relation, in either impressions or ideas, to any of these passions. Thus, suppose we look together at an ordinary stone or another common object that belongs to neither of us and causes no emotion or independent pain or pleasure of its own. It is evident that such an object will produce none of these four passions. Let us try it upon each of them in succession. Let us apply it to love, to hatred, to humility, and to pride; none of them ever arises in the smallest degree imaginable. Let us change the object as often as we please, provided we still choose one that has neither of these two relations. Let us repeat the experiment in all the dispositions of which the mind is capable. No object in the vast variety of nature will, in any disposition,

<margin>
On love

**SECOND EXPERIMENT.** Since an object that lacks both of these relations can never produce any passion, let us give it only one of these relations and see what will follow. Thus, suppose I look at a stone or any common object that belongs either to me or my companion, and by that means it acquires a relation of ideas to the object of the passions: it is clear that, considering the matter *a priori*, no emotion of any kind can reasonably be expected. For besides the fact that a relation of ideas operates secretly and calmly on the mind, it gives an equal impulse toward the opposite passions of pride and humility, or love and hatred, depending on whether the object belongs to ourselves or others; this opposition of the passions must destroy both and leave the mind perfectly free from any affection or emotion. This *a priori* reasoning is confirmed by experience. No trivial or common object that does not cause pain or pleasure independent of the passion will ever, through its ownership or other relations to ourselves or others, be able to produce the affections of pride, humility, love, or hatred.

*Experiments to confirm this system.*

### THIRD EXPERIMENT.

It is evident, therefore, that a relation of ideas alone is not able to give rise to these affections. Let us now remove this relation, and in its place put a relation of impressions, by presenting an object which is agreeable or disagreeable but has no relation either to ourselves or our companion; and let us observe the consequences. To consider the matter first *a priori*, as in the preceding experiment, we may conclude that the object will have a small but uncertain connection with these passions. For besides the fact that this relation is not a cold and imperceptible one, it does not have the inconvenience of the relation of ideas, nor does it direct us with equal force to two contrary passions which by their opposition destroy each other. But if we consider, on the other hand, that this transition from the sensation to the affection is not promoted by any principle that produces a transition of ideas; but, on the contrary, that though the one impression is easily transfused into the other, yet the change of objects is supposed to be contrary to all the principles that cause a transition of that kind; we may from there infer that nothing will ever be a steady or durable cause of any passion that is connected with the passion merely by a relation

What our reason would conclude from analogy, after balancing these arguments, would be that an object which produces pleasure or uneasiness, but has no manner of connection either with ourselves or others, may give such a turn to the disposition that it may naturally fall into pride or love, humility or hatred, and search for other objects upon which, by a double relation, it can base these affections; but that an object which has only one of these relations, though it be the most advantageous one, can never give rise to any constant and established passion.

Most fortunately, all this reasoning is found to be exactly conformable to experience and the phenomena of the passions. Suppose I were traveling with a companion through a country to which we are both utter strangers; it is evident that if the prospects are beautiful, the roads agreeable, and the inns comfortable, this may put me into a good mood both with myself and my fellow traveler. **FOURTH EXPERIMENT.** Having found that neither an object without any relation of ideas or impressions, nor an object that has only one relation, can ever cause pride or humility, love or hatred; reason alone may convince us, without any further experiment, that whatever has a double relation must necessarily excite these passions; since it is evident they must have some cause. But to leave as little room for doubt as possible, let us renew our experiments, and see whether the event in this case answers our expectation. I choose an object, such as virtue, that causes a separate satisfaction: On this object I bestow a relation to self; and find, that from this disposition of affairs, there immediately arises a passion. But what passion? That very one of pride, to which this object bears a double relation. Its idea is related to that of self, the object of the passion: The sensation it causes resembles the sensation of the passion. That I may be sure I am not mistaken in this experiment, I remove first one relation; then

The person has a relation of ideas to myself, according to the supposition; the passion, of which he is the object, by being either agreeable or uneasy, has a relation of impressions to pride or humility. It is evident, then, that one of these passions must arise from the love or hatred.

THIS is the reasoning I form in conformity to my hypothesis; and I am pleased to find upon trial that everything answers exactly to my expectation. The virtue or vice of a son or brother not only excites love or hatred, but by a new transition, from similar causes, gives rise to pride or humility. Nothing causes greater vanity than any shining quality in our relations; just as nothing mortifies us more than their vice or infamy. This exact conformity of experience to our reasoning is a convincing proof of the solidity of that hypothesis upon which we reason.

Suppose that instead of the virtue or vice of a son or brother, which causes first love or hatred and afterwards pride or humility, we place these good or bad qualities in ourselves, without any immediate connection with the person who is related to us: Experience shows us that by this change of situation the whole chain is broken, and that the mind is not conveyed from one passion to another as in the preceding instance. We never love or hate a son or brother for the virtue or vice we discern in ourselves; though it is evident the same qualities in him give us a very sensible pride or humility. The transition from pride or humility to love or hatred is not as natural as from love or hatred to pride or humility. This may at first sight be esteemed contrary to my hypothesis; since the relations of impressions and ideas are in both cases precisely the same. Pride and humility are impressions related to love and hatred. I myself am related to the person. It should, therefore, be expected that like causes must produce like effects, and a perfect transition arise from the double relation, as in all other cases. This difficulty we may easily solve by the following reflections.

<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>
<sig>VOL. II. I</sig>
<meta>catchword: other,</meta>

<margin>
SECTION II.
~
*Experiments to confirm this system.*
</margin>

It is evident that as we are at all times intimately conscious of ourselves, our sentiments, and passions, their ideas must strike us with greater vivacity than the ideas of the sentiments and passions of any other person. But everything that strikes us with vivacity, and appears in a full and strong light, forces itself, in a way, into our consideration, and becomes present to the mind on the smallest hint and most trivial relation. For the same reason, when it is once present, it engages the attention and keeps it from wandering to other objects, however strong their relation to our first object may be. The imagination passes easily from obscure to lively ideas, but with difficulty from lively to obscure. In the one case, the relation is aided by another principle; in the other case, it is opposed by it.

Now I have observed that those two faculties of the mind, the imagination and passions, assist each other in their operation when their propensities are similar, and when they act upon the same object. The mind always has a propensity to pass from a passion to any other related to it; and this propensity is promoted when the object of one passion is related to that of the other. The two impulses concur with each other,

<summary>Hume explains that because the idea of the self is always present and vivid, the mind easily moves toward it but has difficulty moving away from it toward more obscure ideas of others. This principle of vivacity influences how the imagination and passions interact when their objects and propensities are related.</summary>
<keywords>vivacity, self, passions, imagination, propensity, mental faculties, relation of ideas</keywords>

other, and render the whole transition more smooth and easy. But if it should happen that while the relation of ideas, strictly speaking, remains the same, its influence in causing a transition of the imagination should no longer take place, it is evident its influence on the passions must also cease, as it is dependent entirely on that transition. This is the reason why pride or humility is not transfused into love or hatred with the same ease that the latter passions are changed into the former. If a person is my brother, I am his likewise: But though the relations are reciprocal, they have very different effects on the imagination. The passage is smooth and open from the consideration of any person related to us to that of ourselves, of whom we are every moment conscious. But when the affections are once directed to ourselves, the imagination does not pass with the same facility from that object to any other person, however closely connected they are with us. This easy or difficult transition of the imagination operates upon the passions and facilitates or retards their transition; which is a clear proof that these two faculties of the passions and imagination are connected together, and that the relations of ideas have an influence upon the affections.

<summary>Hume argues that the transition from pride/humility to love/hatred is difficult because the imagination moves easily from others to the self, but resists moving away from the vivid idea of the self to others. This demonstrates that the transition of passions is directly dependent on the ease with which the imagination moves between objects.</summary>

Experiments to confirm this system.

affections. Besides innumerable experiments that prove this, we here find that even when the relation remains, if its usual effect upon the imagination in producing an association or transition of ideas is prevented by any particular circumstance, its usual effect upon the passions—in conveying us from one to another—is in like manner prevented.

Some may, perhaps, find a contradiction between this phenomenon and that of sympathy, where the mind passes easily from the idea of ourselves to that of any other object related to us. But this difficulty will vanish if we consider that in sympathy, our own person is not the object of any passion, nor is there anything that fixes our attention on ourselves, as in the present case where we are supposed to be actuated by pride or humility. The self, independent of the perception of every other object, is in reality nothing; for which reason we must turn our view to external objects, and it is natural for us to consider with most attention those that lie near to us or resemble us. But when the self is the object of a passion, it is not natural to quit the consideration of it until the passion is exhausted; in which case the double relations of impressions and ideas can no longer operate.

**PART II.**

**SEVENTH EXPERIMENT.** To put this whole reasoning to a further trial, let us make a new experiment; and as we have already seen the effects of related passions and ideas, let us here suppose an identity of passions along with a relation of ideas, and let us consider the effects of this new situation. It is evident that a transition of the passions from one object to the other is reasonably to be expected here; since the relation of ideas is supposed still to continue, and an identity of impressions must produce a stronger connection than the most perfect resemblance that can be imagined. If a double relation, therefore, of impressions and ideas is able to produce a transition from one to the other, how much more so an identity of impressions with a relation of ideas. Accordingly, we find that when we either love or hate any person, the passions seldom continue within their first bounds; but extend themselves toward all the neighboring objects, and include the friends and relations of him we love or hate. Nothing is more natural than to feel kindness toward one brother on account of our friendship for another, without any further examination of his character. A quarrel with one person gives us a hatred for the whole family,

<summary>Hume introduces a new experiment to show that passions easily transfer from one person to their associates, such as friends or family, because of the close relation of ideas and the continuity of the emotion. He observes that our love or hatred for an individual rarely remains isolated but naturally extends to those connected to them.</summary>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>
<sig>I 3</sig>

though entirely innocent of that which displeases us. Instances of this kind are everywhere to be met with.

<margin>
SECTION II.
~~~
*Experiments to confirm this system.*
</margin>
THERE is only one difficulty in this experiment, which it will be necessary to account for before we proceed any further. It is evident that though all passions pass easily from one object to another related to it, yet this transition is made with greater facility where the more considerable object is first presented and the lesser follows it, than where this order is reversed and the lesser takes the precedence. Thus it is more natural for us to love the son on account of the father, than the father on account of the son; the servant for the master, than the master for the servant; the subject for the prince, than the prince for the subject. In like manner, we more readily contract a hatred against a whole family where our first quarrel is with the head of it, than where we are displeased with a son, or servant, or some inferior member. In short, our passions, like other objects, descend with greater facility than they ascend.

THAT we may comprehend wherein the difficulty of explaining this phenomenon consists, we must consider that the very same reason which determines the imagi-

<summary>Hume notes that passions transfer more easily from a superior or more significant object to an inferior one than in the opposite direction. He observes that it is more natural for love or hatred to "descend" through a hierarchy of relations, such as from a father to a son.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hume’s discussion of the asymmetry in how the mind and passions transition between objects of different importance, specifically why the imagination moves more easily from a lesser object to a greater one.</meta>

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>A Treatise of Human Nature.</header>

<margin>

---
Of love and hatred.
</margin>
imagination to pass from remote to contiguous objects with more facility than from contiguous to remote, causes it likewise to change with more ease from the lesser to the greater, than from the greater to the lesser. Whatever has the greatest influence is most noticed; and whatever is most noticed presents itself most readily to the imagination. We are more apt to overlook in any subject what is trivial than what appears of considerable moment; but especially if the latter takes precedence and first engages our attention. Thus, if any accident makes us consider the *satellites* of *Jupiter*, our fancy is naturally determined to form the idea of that planet; but if we first reflect on the principal planet, it is more natural for us to overlook its attendants. The mention of the provinces of any empire conveys our thought to the seat of the empire; but the fancy returns not with the same facility to the consideration of the provinces. The idea of the servant makes us think of the master; that of the subject carries our view to the prince. But the same relation has not an equal influence in conveying us back again. And on this is founded that reproach of *Cornelia* to her sons, that they ought to be ashamed she

He uses examples like Jupiter's satellites, imperial provinces, and servants to show how our thoughts are drawn more easily to the "greater" object in a relationship.</summary>

This was, in other words, exhorting them to make themselves as illustrious and famous as their grandfather; otherwise, the imagination of the people—passing from her, who was intermediate and placed in an equal relation to both—would always leave them and identify her by what was more considerable and of greater importance. On the same principle is founded the common custom of making wives bear the name of their husbands rather than husbands that of their wives, as well as the ceremony of giving precedence to those whom we honor and respect. We might find many other instances to confirm this principle, if it were not already sufficiently evident.

Now, since the imagination finds the same ease in passing from the lesser to the greater as from the remote to the contiguous, why does this easy transition of ideas not assist the transition of passions in the former case as well as in the latter? The virtues of a friend or brother produce first love and then pride because, in that case, the imagination passes from the remote to the contiguous according to its natural inclination. But the love or hatred of an inferior does not readily cause any passion toward the superior, even though that is the natural propensity of the imagination; whereas the love or hatred of a superior causes a passion toward the inferior, contrary to its propensity. In short, the same facility of transition does not operate in the same manner upon the superior and inferior as it does upon the contiguous and remote. These two phenomena appear contradictory and require some attention to be reconciled.

As the transition of ideas is here made contrary to the natural propensity of the imagination, that faculty must be overpowered by some stronger principle of another kind; and as nothing is ever present to the mind but impressions and ideas, this principle must necessarily lie in the impressions. Now it has been observed that impressions or passions are connected only by their resemblance, and that where any two passions place the mind in the same or in similar dispositions, it very naturally passes from the one to the other; just as, on the contrary, a repugnance in the dispositions produces a difficulty in the transition.

<summary>Hume examines why passions do not always transition between superior and inferior objects in the same way they do between contiguous ones. He suggests that when the imagination's natural movement is hindered, the transition must be governed by the resemblance of impressions.</summary>

transition of the passions. But it is observable that this repugnance may arise from a difference of degree as well as of kind; nor do we experience a greater difficulty in passing suddenly from a small degree of *love* to a small degree of *hatred* than from a *small* to a *great* degree of either of these affections. A man, when calm or only moderately agitated, is so different in every respect from himself when disturbed with a violent passion, that no two people can be more unlike; nor is it easy to pass from the one extreme to the other without a considerable interval between them.

The difficulty is no less, if it is not even greater, in passing from a *strong* passion to a *weak* one than in passing from a *weak* to a *strong* one, provided that the one passion destroys the other upon its appearance and they do not both exist at once. But the case is entirely altered when the passions unite together and actuate the mind at the same time. A weak passion, when added to a strong one, does not make as significant a change in the disposition as a strong one when added to a weak one; for this reason, there is a closer connection between a great degree and a small one than between a small degree and a great one.

<summary>Hume explains that shifting between different intensities of the same passion can be as difficult as shifting between opposite passions like love and hatred. He notes that while transitions between extremes are difficult, a weak passion can more easily be absorbed into a strong one than vice versa.</summary>

### PART II.

---

<margin>On love and hatred.</margin>

The degree of any passion depends upon the nature of its object; and an affection directed toward a person who is significant in our eyes fills and possesses the mind much more than one which has as its object a person we consider of less importance. Here, then, the contradiction between the tendencies of the imagination and passion displays itself. When we turn our thoughts to a great and a small object, the imagination finds it easier to pass from the small to the great than from the great to the small; but the affections find a greater difficulty. And as the affections are a more powerful principle than the imagination, it is no wonder they prevail over it and draw the mind to their side. In spite of the difficulty of passing from the idea of something great to something small, a passion directed toward the former always produces a similar passion toward the latter when the great and small are related together. The idea of the servant leads our thoughts most readily to the master; but the hatred or love of the master produces, with greater ease, anger or goodwill toward the servant. The strongest passion in this case takes precedence; and the addition of the weaker one makes no significant change to the dispo-

<summary>Hume argues that while the imagination moves easily from small objects to large ones, the passions transition more effectively from significant objects to minor ones. He illustrates this by noting that emotions felt toward a master are more easily transferred to a servant than vice versa.</summary>

Experiments to confirm this system.

As in the previous experiment we found that a relation of ideas—which, by any particular circumstance, ceases to produce its usual effect of facilitating the transition of ideas—also ceases to operate on the passions; so in the present experiment we find the same property of the impressions. Two different degrees of the same passion are surely related together; but if the smaller is present first, it has little or no tendency to introduce the greater; and that is because the addition of the great to the little produces a more noticeable alteration in the temperament than the addition of the little to the great. These phenomena, when duly weighed, will be found to be convincing proofs of this hypothesis.

And these proofs will be confirmed if we consider the manner in which the mind here reconciles the contradiction I have observed between the passions and the imagination. The fancy passes with more facility from the less to the greater than from the greater to the less: But on the contrary, a violent passion produces a feeble one more easily than a feeble one produces a violent one. In this opposition, the passion in the end prevails

When we love the father or master of a family, we think little of his children or servants. But when these are present with us, or when it is in any way in our power to serve them, their nearness and contiguity in this case increase their magnitude, or at least remove that opposition which the fancy makes to the transition of the affections. If the imagination finds it difficult to pass from greater to less, it finds an equal facility in passing from remote to contiguous, which brings the matter to an equality and leaves the way open from one passion to the other.

EIGHTH EXPERIMENT. I have observed that the transition from love or hatred to pride or humility is easier than from pride or humility to love or hatred; and that the difficulty which the imagination finds in passing from contiguous to remote is the reason why we have scarcely any instance of the latter transition of the affections. I must, however, make one exception, *namely*, when the very cause of the pride and humility is placed in some other

Thus, nothing more readily produces kindness and affection toward any person than his approbation of our conduct and character; as, on the other hand, nothing inspires us with a stronger hatred than his blame or contempt. Here it is evident that the original passion is pride or humility, whose object is self; and that this passion is transfused into love or hatred, whose object is some other person, notwithstanding the rule I have already established, *that the imagination passes with difficulty from contiguous to remote*. But the transition in this case is not made merely on account of the relation between ourselves and the person; but because that very person is the real cause of our first passion, and of consequence is intimately connected with it. It is his approbation that produces pride, and his disapprobation, humility. No wonder, then, the imagination returns back again attended with the related passions of love and hatred. This is not a contradiction, but an exception to the rule; and an exception that arises from the same reason with the rule itself.

## SECTION III.

### *Difficulties solved.*

<margin>SECTION III.</margin>
After so many and such undeniable proofs drawn from daily experience and observation, it may seem superfluous to enter into a particular examination of all the causes of love and hatred. I shall, therefore, employ the remainder of this part, *first*, in removing some difficulties concerning particular causes of these passions; and *secondly*, in examining the compound affections which arise from the mixture of love and hatred with other emotions.

Nothing is more evident than that any person acquires our kindness, or is exposed to our ill will, in proportion to the pleasure or discomfort we receive from him, and that the passions keep pace exactly with the sensations in all their changes and variations. When our own nation is at war with any other, we detest them, characterizing them as cruel, treacherous, unjust, and violent; but we always regard ourselves and our allies as fair, moderate, and merciful. If the enemy general is successful, it is only with difficulty that we grant him the form and character of a man. He is a sorcerer; he has dealings with demons, as was reported of *Oliver Cromwell* and the *Duke of Luxembourg*. He is bloodthirsty and takes pleasure in death and destruction. But if the success is on our side, our commander possesses all the opposite good qualities and is a model of virtue, as well as of courage and leadership. We call his treachery "strategy"; his cruelty is an evil inseparable from war. In short, we either try to downplay each of his faults or dignify them with the name of the virtue that they most closely resemble. It is evident that the same way of thinking prevails throughout common life.

There are some who add another condition and require not only that the pain and pleasure arise from the person, but likewise

A man who wounds and harms us by accident does not become our enemy on that account, nor do we think ourselves bound by any ties of gratitude to one who does us any service in the same manner. We judge actions by the intention, and according to whether that intention is good or bad, they become causes of love or hatred.

But here we must make a distinction. If a quality in another person that pleases or displeases is constant and inherent in their person and character, it will cause love or hatred regardless of the intention. Otherwise, knowledge and design are required in order to give rise to these passions. Someone who is disagreeable because of their deformity or folly is the object of our aversion, though nothing is more certain than that they have not the least intention of displeasing us by these qualities. But if the uneasiness does not proceed from a quality, but from an action which is produced and vanished in a moment, it is necessary—in order to create some relationship and connect this action sufficiently with the person—that it be derived from a particular forethought and design. It does not reach the sensing and thinking part, and it neither proceeds from anything lasting in him nor leaves anything behind; instead, it passes in a moment and is as if it had never existed. On the other hand, an intention shows certain qualities which, remaining after the action is performed, connect it with the person and facilitate the transition of ideas from one to the other. We can never think of him without reflecting on these qualities, unless repentance and a change of life have produced an alteration in that regard—in which case the passion is likewise altered. This, therefore, is one reason why an intention is required to excite either love or hatred.

BUT we must further consider that an intention, besides strengthening the relationship of ideas, is often necessary to produce a relationship of impressions and give rise to pleasure and uneasiness. For it is observable that the principal part of an injury is the contempt and hatred that it shows in the person who injures us; and without that, the mere harm gives us a less noticeable uneasiness.

To illustrate this theory by a parallel instance, we may observe that not only does the discomfort which proceeds from another by accident have little force to excite our passion, but also that which arises from an acknowledged necessity and duty. One who has a real intention of harming us, proceeding not from hatred and ill-will but from justice and fairness, does not draw our anger upon themselves if we are in any degree reasonable; despite the fact that they are both the cause, and the intentional cause, of our sufferings. It is evident, in the first place, that this circumstance is not decisive; and though it may be able to diminish the passions, it is seldom that it can entirely remove them. BESIDES, we may consider that when we receive harm from any person, we are apt to imagine him criminal, and it is with extreme difficulty that we allow for his justice and innocence. This is a clear proof that, independent of the opinion of iniquity, any harm or uneasiness has a natural tendency to excite our hatred, and that we afterward seek for reasons upon which we may justify and establish the passion. Here the idea of injury does not produce the passion, but arises from it.

NOR is it any wonder that passion should produce the opinion of injury; since otherwise it must suffer a considerable diminution, which all the passions avoid as much as possible. The removal of injury may remove the anger without proving that the anger arises only from the injury. The harm and the justice are two contrary objects, of which the one has a tendency to produce hatred, and the other love; and it is according to their different degrees, and our particular turn of thinking, that either

# SECTION IV.
## *Of the love of relations.*

<margin>SECT. IV.</margin>
HAVING given a reason why several actions that cause a real pleasure or uneasiness excite no degree, or but a small one, of the passion of love or hatred toward the actors, it will be necessary to show wherein consists the pleasure or uneasiness of many objects which we find by experience to produce these passions.

ACCORDING to the preceding system, there is always required a double relation of impressions and ideas between the cause and effect, in order to produce either love or hatred. But though this is universally true, it is remarkable that the passion of love may be excited by only one *relation* of a different kind, *namely*, between ourselves and the object; or more properly speaking, that this relation is always attended with both the others. Whoever is united to us by any connection is always sure of a share of our love, proportioned to the connection, without inquiring into his other qualities. Thus the

<summary>Hume transitions to discussing how personal relationships and connections between individuals naturally produce love. He argues that a simple relation between ourselves and another person is sufficient to trigger the double relation of impressions and ideas required to excite the passion of love.</summary>
<keywords>love, hatred, relations, double relation, impressions, ideas, connection, human nature, sympathy</keywords>

### SECT. IV.
*Of the love of relations.*
</margin>
the relation of blood produces the strongest tie the mind is capable of in the love of parents to their children, and a lesser degree of the same affection as the relation lessens. Nor does consanguinity alone have this effect, but any other relation without exception. We love our countrymen, our neighbors, those of the same trade, profession, and even name as ourselves. Every one of these relations is esteemed some tie, and gives a title to a share of our affection.

THERE is another phenomenon which is parallel to this, *namely*, that *acquaintance*, without any kind of relation, gives rise to love and kindness. When we have contracted a habit and intimacy with any person; though in frequenting his company we have not been able to discover any very valuable quality that he possesses; yet we cannot help preferring him to strangers whose superior merit we are fully convinced of. These two phenomena of the effects of relation and acquaintance will give mutual light to each other, and may both be explained from the same principle.

From this, they say, proceeds that continual search after amusement in gaming, in hunting, in business; by which we endeavor to forget ourselves, and excite our spirits from the languid state into which they fall when not sustained by some brisk and lively emotion. To this method of thinking I so far agree that I admit the mind to be insufficient, of itself, for its own entertainment, and that it naturally seeks after foreign objects which may produce a lively sensation and agitate the spirits. On the appearance of such an object, it awakes, as it were, from a dream: the blood flows with a new tide; the heart is elevated; and the whole man acquires a vigor which he cannot command in his solitary and calm moments. Hence company is naturally so joyful, as it presents the liveliest of all objects, *namely*, a rational and thinking Being like ourselves, who communicates to us all the actions of his mind; makes us privy to his inmost sentiments and affections; and lets us see, in the very instant of their production, all the emotions which are caused by any object. SECTION IV.
---
*Of the love of relations.*
</margin>
ONCE this is admitted, all the rest is easy. For just as the company of strangers is agreeable to us for *a short time* by enlivening our thoughts, so the company of our relations and acquaintances must be particularly agreeable because it has this effect to a greater degree and has a more *durable* influence. Whatever is related to us is conceived in a lively manner by the easy transition from ourselves to the related object. Custom also, or acquaintance, facilitates the introduction and strengthens the conception of any object. The first case is parallel to our reasoning from cause and effect; the second to education. And as reasoning and education agree only in producing a lively and strong idea of any object, so is this the only detail which is common to relation and acquaintance. This must, therefore, be the influencing quality by which they produce all their common effects; and since love or kindness is one of these effects, the passion must be derived from the force and liveliness of the conception. Such a conception is particularly agreeable, and

He argues that this liveliness of conception, strengthened by custom and relation, is the source of feelings like love and kindness.</summary>

It is obvious that people associate together according to their particular tempers and dispositions, and that men of cheerful tempers naturally love those who are cheerful, just as the serious feel an affection for the serious. This happens not only when they notice this resemblance between themselves and others, but also through the natural course of the disposition and by a certain sympathy that always arises between similar characters. Where they notice the resemblance, it operates in the manner of a relation by producing a connection of ideas. Where they do not notice it, it operates by some other principle; and if this latter principle is similar to the former, it must be received as a confirmation of the previous reasoning.

THE idea of ourselves is always intimately present to us and conveys a noticeable degree of vivacity to the idea of any other object to which we are related. This lively idea changes by degrees into a real impression; these two kinds of perception are in a great measure the same and differ only in their degrees of force and vivacity. But this change must be produced

The great propensity men have for pride may be considered as another similar phenomenon. It often happens that after we have lived a considerable time in any city, however disagreeable it might have been to us at first, yet as we become familiar with the objects and form an acquaintance, though merely with the streets and buildings, the aversion diminishes by degrees and at last changes into the opposite passion. ## PART II.

<margin>Of love and hatred.</margin>

It may not be amiss, in treating the affection we bear our acquaintances and relations, to observe some quite curious phenomena which attend it. It is easy to remark in common life that children believe their relation to their mother to be weakened, in a great measure, by her second marriage, and no longer regard her with the same eye as if she had continued in her state of widowhood. Nor does this happen only when they have felt any inconveniences from her second marriage, or when her husband is much her inferior; but even without any of these considerations, and merely because she has become part of another family. This also takes place with regard to the second marriage of a father, but in a much lesser degree; and it is certain the ties of blood are not so much loosened in the latter case as by the marriage of a mother. ### SECTION IV.

---

<margin>
Of the love of relations.
</margin>
IN order to produce a perfect relation between two objects, it is requisite, not only that the imagination be conveyed from one to the other by resemblance, contiguity, or causation, but also that it return back from the second to the first with the same ease and facility. At first sight, this may seem a necessary and unavoidable consequence. If one object resembles another, the latter object must necessarily resemble the former. If one object is the cause of another, the second object is the effect of its cause. It is the same case with contiguity: And therefore, the relation being always reciprocal, it may be thought that the return of the imagination from the second to the first must also, in every case, be as natural as its passage from the first to the second. But upon further examination, we shall easily discover our mistake. For supposing the second object, beside its reciprocal relation to the first, to have also a strong relation to a third object; in that case the thought, passing from the first object to the second, returns not back with the same facility, though the relation continues the same; but is readily carried on to the third object, by means

<summary>Hume argues that for a "perfect relation" to exist between two objects, the mind must be able to move between them in both directions with equal ease. He notes that this reciprocity is often interrupted when the second object is also strongly related to a third object, drawing the imagination away.</summary>

of the new relation, which presents itself, and gives a new impulse to the imagination. This new relation, therefore, weakens the tie between the first and second objects. The fancy is by its very nature wavering and inconstant; and always considers two objects as more strongly related together where it finds the passage equally easy both in going and returning, than where the transition is easy only in one of these motions. The double motion is a kind of a double tie, and binds the objects together in the closest and most intimate manner.

THE second marriage of a mother does not break the relation of child and parent; and that relation suffices to convey my imagination from myself to her with the greatest ease and facility. But after the imagination has arrived at this point of view, it finds its object to be surrounded with so many other relations, which challenge its regard, that it knows not which to prefer, and is at a loss what new object to pitch upon. The ties of interest and duty bind her to another family, and prevent that return of the fancy from her to myself, which is necessary to support the union. The thought no longer has the vibration, requisite to set it per-

<meta>This page continues David Hume's argument regarding the "vibration" of the imagination between related objects. It specifically addresses why a father's second marriage does not weaken the child-parent bond as much as a mother's second marriage does.</meta>

SECT. IV.

*Of the love of relations.*

fectly at ease, and indulge its inclination to change. It goes with facility, but returns with difficulty; and by that interruption finds the relation much weakened from what it would be were the passage open and easy on both sides.

Now to give a reason why this effect does not follow in the same degree upon the second marriage of a father: we may reflect on what has been proved already, that though the imagination goes easily from the view of a lesser object to that of a greater, yet it does not return with the same facility from the greater to the less. When my imagination goes from myself to my father, it does not pass so readily from him to his second wife, nor does it consider him as entering into a different family, but as continuing the head of that family of which I am myself a part. His superiority prevents the easy transition of the thought from him to his spouse, but keeps the passage still open for a return to myself along the same relation of child and parent. He is not lost in the new relation he acquires; so that the double motion or vibration of thought is still easy and natural. By this indulgence of the fancy in its inconstancy, the tie of child and parent still preserves its full force and influence.

This maintains the "vibration of thought" necessary to preserve the strength of the relationship.</summary>

### SECTION V.

#### *Of our esteem for the rich and powerful.*

<margin>
SECT. V.
<image-desc>horizontal wavy line</image-desc>

Nothing has a greater tendency to give us esteem for any person than his power and riches, or contempt than his poverty and meanness. And as esteem and contempt are to be considered as types of love and hatred, it will be proper in this place to explain these phenomena.

Here it happens most fortunately that the greatest difficulty is not to discover a principle capable of producing such an effect, but to choose the chief and predominant one among several that present themselves. The *satisfaction* we take in the riches of others,

<margin>Section V. Of our esteem for the rich and powerful</margin>
*First*, to the objects they possess, such as houses, gardens, and carriages; which, being agreeable in themselves, necessarily produce a feeling of pleasure in everyone who either considers or views them. *Secondly*, to the expectation of benefit from the rich and powerful through our sharing in their possessions. *Thirdly*, to sympathy, which makes us share in the satisfaction of everyone who approaches us. All these principles may work together in producing this phenomenon. The question is, to which of them we ought principally to attribute it.

It is certain that the first principle, *namely*, the reflection on agreeable objects, has a greater influence than what we might be inclined to imagine at first sight. We seldom reflect on what is beautiful or ugly, agreeable or disagreeable, without an emotion of pleasure or unease; and though these sensations do not appear much in our common, lazy way of thinking, it is easy to discover them in either reading or conversation. Men of wit always turn the conversation to subjects that are entertaining to the imagination, and poets never present any objects except those of the same nature. Mr.

Philips has chosen *Cyder* for the subject of an excellent poem. Beer would not have been as suitable, as it is neither as pleasing to the taste nor to the eye. But he would certainly have preferred wine to either of them, could his native country have provided him with such an agreeable liquor. From this, we may learn that everything which is pleasing to the senses is also, to some extent, pleasing to the imagination, and conveys to the mind an image of that satisfaction which it provides through its actual application to the bodily organs.

BUT though these reasons may lead us to include this refinement of the imagination among the causes of the respect we pay to the rich and powerful, there are many other reasons that may prevent us from regarding it as the sole or primary cause. For since ideas of pleasure can only exert influence through their vivacity—which makes them resemble impressions—it is most natural that those ideas should have that influence which are favored by the most circumstances and have a natural tendency to become strong and lively; such as our ideas of the passions and sensations of any human being. Every human being resembles ourselves, and by that means has an advantage over any other object in affecting the imagination.

<summary>Hume argues that objects pleasing to the senses also please the imagination, but notes that this alone does not explain our respect for the rich. BESIDES, if we consider the nature of that faculty and the great influence which all relations have upon it, we shall easily be persuaded that however the ideas of pleasant wines, music, or gardens, which the rich man enjoys, may become lively and agreeable, the imagination will not confine itself to them, but will carry its view to related objects; and in particular, to the person who possesses them. And this is the more natural because the pleasant idea or image produces here a passion toward the person by means of his relation to the object; so that it is unavoidable that he must enter into the original conception, since he is the object of the derivative passion. But if he enters into the original conception and is considered as enjoying these agreeable objects, it is *sympathy* which is properly the cause of the affection; and the *third* principle is more powerful and universal than the *first*.

ADD to this that riches and power alone, even though unemployed, naturally cause esteem and respect. Consequently, these passions do not arise from the idea of any beautiful or agreeable objects. <margin>SECTION 5. — *Of our esteem for the rich and powerful.*</margin>
But we do not need to look as far as prisoners of war and the dead to find instances of this disinterested esteem for riches; let us observe with a little attention those phenomena that occur to us in common life and conversation. A man, who possesses a competent fortune himself, upon entering a company of strangers, naturally treats them with different degrees of respect and deference as he is informed of their different fortunes and conditions. This happens even though it is impossible that he could ever propose to gain—and perhaps would not even accept—any advantage from them. A traveler is always admitted into company and meets with civility in proportion to how his train and equipage <note>His retinue, staff, and traveling equipment</note> show him to be a man of great or moderate fortune. In short, the different ranks of men are, in a great measure, regulated by riches, and that is true with regard to superiors as well as inferiors, and strangers as well as acquaintances.

tection from the rich and powerful, and to esteem them on that account, we extend the same sentiments to those who resemble them in their fortune, but from whom we can never hope for any advantage. The general rule still prevails, and by giving a direction to the imagination, it draws the passion along with it, in the same manner as if its proper object were real and existing.

**B**ut that this principle does not apply here will easily appear if we consider that, in order to establish a general rule and extend it beyond its proper bounds, there is required a certain uniformity in our experience and a great superiority of those instances that conform to the rule over those that are contrary to it. But here the case is quite different. Of a hundred men of credit and fortune I meet, there is not, perhaps, one from whom I can expect any advantage; so it is impossible that any custom could ever prevail in the present case.

**U**pon the whole, nothing remains that can give us an esteem for power and riches, and a contempt for lowliness <note>Social insignificance or low status</note> and poverty, except the principle of sympathy, by which we enter into the sentiments of the rich and poor, and partake of their pleasure and uneasiness. From this relation of impressions and identity of ideas, the passion arises, according to my hypothesis.

**T**HE best method of reconciling us to this opinion is to take a general survey of the universe and observe the force of sympathy through the whole animal creation, and the easy communication of sentiments from one thinking being to another. In all creatures that do not prey upon others and are not agitated by violent passions, there appears a remarkable desire for company, which associates them together without any advantages they could ever expect to gain from their union. This is still more conspicuous in man, as the creature in the universe who has the most ardent desire for society and is fitted for it by the most advantages. We can form no wish that does not have a reference to society. A perfect solitude is, perhaps, the greatest punishment

Whatever other passions we may be driven by—pride, ambition, greed, curiosity, revenge, or lust—the soul or animating principle of them all is sympathy; nor would they have any force if we were to separate them entirely from the thoughts and sentiments of others. Let all the powers and elements of nature conspire to serve and obey one man: Let the sun rise and set at his command; let the sea and rivers flow as he pleases, and let the earth spontaneously provide whatever may be useful or agreeable to him. He will still be miserable until you give him at least one person with whom he may share his happiness, and whose esteem and friendship he may enjoy.

**THIS** conclusion from a general view of human nature can be confirmed by particular instances in which the force of sympathy is very remarkable. The observation of convenience gives pleasure, since convenience is a beauty. But in what manner does it give pleasure? It is certain our own interest is not in the least concerned; and as this is a beauty of interest, not of form, so to speak, it must delight us merely by communication, and by our sympathizing with the proprietor of the lodging. We enter into his interest by the force of imagination, and feel the same satisfaction that the objects naturally cause in him.

This observation extends to tables, chairs, writing desks, chimneys, coaches, saddles, plows, and indeed to every work of art; it being a universal rule that their beauty is chiefly derived from their utility, and from their fitness for that purpose to which they are destined. But this is an advantage that concerns only the owner, nor is there anything but sympathy which can interest the spectator.

It is evident that nothing renders a field more agreeable than its fertility, and that scarcely any advantages of ornament or situation will be able to equal this beauty. It is the same case with particular trees and plants as with the field on which they grow. I do not know but that a plain, overgrown with furze and broom, may be, in itself, as beautiful as a hill covered with vines or olive trees; though it will never appear so to one who is acquainted with the value of each. But this is a beauty merely of imagination and has no foundation in what appears to the senses. Fertility and value have a plain reference to use, and that to riches, joy, and plenty; in which, though we have no hope of partaking, yet we enter into them by the vivacity of the fancy and share them, in some measure, with the proprietor.

In general, we may remark that the minds of men are mirrors to one another, not only because they reflect each other's emotions, but also because those rays of passions, sentiments, and opinions may often be reverberated, and may decay away by imperceptible degrees. Thus, the pleasure which a rich man receives from his possessions, being projected onto the beholder, causes pleasure and esteem; these sentiments, in turn, being perceived and sympathized with, increase the pleasure of the possessor; and being once more reflected, they become a new foundation for pleasure and esteem in the beholder. There is certainly an original satisfaction in riches derived from that power which they bestow of enjoying all the pleasures of life; and as this is their very nature and essence, it must be the primary source of all the passions that arise from them. One of the most considerable of these passions is that of

On the other hand, impressions and passions are capable of a complete union; and like colors, they may be blended so perfectly together that each of them may lose its individual identity and contribute only to vary that uniform impression which arises from the whole. Some of the most curious phenomena of the human mind are derived from this property of the passions.

In examining those ingredients which are capable of uniting with love and hatred, I begin to become aware, in some measure, of a misfortune that has affected every system of philosophy with which the world has yet been acquainted. It is commonly found that in accounting for the operations of nature by any particular hypothesis, among a number of experiments that align exactly with the principles we would endeavor to establish, there is always some phenomenon which is more stubborn and will not so easily bend to our purpose. But as the perceptions of the mind are perfectly known, and I have used all imaginable caution in forming conclusions concerning them, I have always hoped to keep clear of those contradictions which have accompanied every other system. Accordingly, the difficulty which I am currently considering is in no way contrary to my system; it only departs a little from that simplicity which has been its principal strength and beauty until now.

The passions of *love* and *hatred* are always followed by, or rather joined with, *benevolence* and *anger*. It is this conjunction which chiefly distinguishes these affections from *pride* and *humility*. For *pride* and *humility* are pure emotions in the soul, unaccompanied by any desire and not immediately prompting us to action. But *love* and *hatred* are not completed within themselves, nor do they rest in the emotion which they produce, but they carry the mind to something further. *Love* is always followed by a desire for the happiness of the beloved person and an aversion to their misery; just as *hatred* produces a desire for the misery and an aversion to the happiness of the person hated. Such a remarkable difference between these two
