<margin>SECTION VI. <br/> Of benevolence and anger.</margin>
THE connection of this desire and aversion with love and hatred may be explained by two different hypotheses. The first is, that love and hatred have not only a *cause*, which excites them, namely pleasure and pain; and an *object*, to which they are directed, namely a person or thinking being; but likewise an *end*, which they endeavor to attain, namely the happiness or misery of the person beloved or hated; all these views, mixing together, make only one passion. According to this system, love is nothing but the desire for another person's happiness, and hatred that of misery. The desire and aversion constitute the very nature of love and hatred. They are not only inseparable but identical.

BUT this is evidently contrary to experience. For though it is certain we never love any person without desiring his happiness, nor hate any without wishing his misery, yet these desires arise only when the ideas of the happiness or misery of our friend or enemy are presented by the imagination,

WE may, therefore, infer, that benevolence and anger are passions different from love and hatred, and only joined with them by the original constitution of the mind. As nature has given to the body certain appetites and inclinations, which she increases, diminishes, or changes according to the situation of the fluids or solids; she has proceeded in the same manner with the mind. According as we are possessed with love or hatred, the corresponding desire of the happiness or misery of the person, who is the object of these passions, arises in the mind, and varies with each variation of these opposite passions. It will be easy to explain the passion of *pity* from the preceding reasoning concerning *sympathy*. We have a lively idea of everything related to us. All human creatures are related to us by resemblance. Their persons, therefore, their interests, their passions, their pains, and their pleasures must strike us in a lively manner and produce an emotion similar to the original one; since a lively idea is easily converted into an impression. If this is true in general, it must be even more true of affliction and sorrow. These always have a stronger and more lasting influence than any pleasure or enjoyment.

I am at least sure that this method of reasoning would be considered certain, either in natural philosophy or common life.

Those philosophers, who derive this passion from I know not what subtle reflections on the instability of fortune, and our being liable to the same miseries we behold, will find this observation contrary to them among a great many others, which would be easy to produce.

There remains only to take notice of a pretty remarkable phenomenon of this passion; which is, that the communicated passion of sympathy sometimes acquires strength from the weakness of its original, and even arises by a transition from affections which have no existence. Thus when a person obtains any honorable office, or inherits a great fortune, we are always the more rejoiced for his prosperity the less sense he seems to have of it, and the greater equanimity and indifference he shows in its enjoyment. In like manner a man, who is not dejected by misfortunes, is the more lamented on account of his patience; and if that virtue extends so far as utterly to remove all sense of uneasiness, it still further increases our compassion. SECTION VII.
<image-desc>decorative flourish</image-desc>
*Of compassion.*
<image-desc>decorative flourish</image-desc>
</margin>
falls into what is commonly considered a great misfortune, we form a notion of his condition; and carrying our imagination from the cause to the usual effect, we first conceive a lively idea of his sorrow, and then feel an impression of it, entirely overlooking that greatness of mind which elevates him above such emotions, or only considering it so far as to increase our admiration, love, and tenderness for him. We find from experience that such a degree of passion is usually connected with such a misfortune; and though there is an exception in the present case, yet the imagination is affected by the *general rule*, and makes us conceive a lively idea of the passion, or rather feel the passion itself, in the same manner as if the person were really driven by it. From the same principles, we blush for the conduct of those who behave themselves foolishly before us; and that occurs even though they show no sense of shame, nor seem in the least conscious of their folly. All this proceeds from sympathy; but it is of a partial kind, and views its objects only on one side, without considering the other, which has a contrary effect and would entirely destroy that emotion which arises from the first appearance.

</margin>

We also have instances where an indifference and lack of feeling during a misfortune increase our concern for the unfortunate person, even though the indifference does not result from any virtue or greatness of mind. It is an intensification of a murder that it was committed against people who were asleep and in perfect security; as historians frequently observe of any infant prince who is a captive in the hands of his enemies, that he is more worthy of compassion the less aware he is of his miserable condition. Since we ourselves are aware of the wretched situation of the person, it gives us a lively idea and sensation of sorrow, which is the passion that *generally* accompanies it; and this idea becomes even more lively, and the sensation more intense, by a contrast with that security and indifference which we observe in the person himself. A contrast of any kind never fails to affect the imagination, especially when presented by the subject; and it is on the imagination that pity entirely depends <note>To prevent all ambiguity, I must observe that where I oppose the imagination to the memory, I mean in general the faculty that presents our fainter ideas. In all other places, and particularly when it is opposed to the understanding, I mean the same faculty, while excluding only our demonstrative and probable reasonings.</note>.

---

<summary>Hume explains that pity is often intensified by a victim's lack of awareness of their own suffering, as the contrast between their indifference and the observer's knowledge heightens the imaginative response. He clarifies that pity depends on the imagination's ability to project feelings based on general rules of what a situation usually entails.</summary>

We must now proceed to account for the passion of *malice*, which imitates the effects of hatred, as pity does those of love; and gives us a joy in the sufferings and miseries of others, without any offense or injury on their part.

So little are men governed by reason in their sentiments and opinions, that they always judge objects more by comparison than from their intrinsic worth and value. When the mind considers, or is accustomed to, any degree of perfection, whatever falls short of it, though really estimable, nonetheless has the same effect upon the passions as what is defective and bad. This is an *original* quality of the soul, and similar to what we experience every day in our bodies. Let a man heat one hand and cool the other; the same water will, at the same time, seem both hot and cold, according to the disposition of the different organs. A small degree of any quality, following a greater, produces the same sen-

THIS no one can doubt regarding our passions and sensations. But some difficulty may arise regarding our ideas and objects. When an object grows or shrinks to the eye or imagination due to a comparison with others, the image and idea of the object remain the same, and are equally extended on the *retina*, and in the brain or organ of perception. The eyes refract the rays of light, and the optic nerves convey the images to the brain in exactly the same manner, whether a large or small object has preceded it; nor does even the imagination alter the dimensions of its object because of a comparison with others. When an object is found by experience to be always accompanied by another, whenever the first object appears, though changed in very material circumstances, we naturally fly to the conception of the second and form an idea of it in as lively and strong a manner as if we had inferred its existence by the most accurate and authentic conclusion of our understanding. Nothing can undeceive us, not even our senses, which, instead of correcting this false judgment, are often perverted by it and seem to authorize its errors.

The conclusion I draw from these two principles, joined with the influence of comparison mentioned above, is very short and decisive. Every object is attended by some emotion proportioned to it; a great object with a great emotion, a small object with a small emotion. A great *object*, therefore, succeeding a small one makes a great *emotion* succeed a small one. Now, a great emotion succeeding a small one becomes still greater and rises beyond its ordinary proportion. We have so many instances of this that it is impossible we can dispute its truth; and it is from this principle I derive the passions of malice and envy.

Now, as we seldom judge objects by their intrinsic value, but form our notions of them by comparison with other objects; it follows that, as we observe a greater or lesser share of happiness or misery in others, we must make an estimate of our own and feel a consequent pain or pleasure. The misery of another gives us a more lively idea of our happiness, and his happiness a more lively idea of our misery. The former, therefore, produces delight, and the latter produces uneasiness.

HERE, then, is a kind of reversed pity, or contrary sensations arising in the beholder from those which are felt by the person whom he considers. In general, we may observe that in all kinds of comparison, an object always makes us receive from another to which it is compared a sensation contrary to what arises from itself in its direct and immediate survey. A small object makes a great one appear even greater. A great object makes a little one appear smaller. Deformity, by itself, produces uneasiness, but makes us receive new pleasure by its contrast with a beautiful object, whose beauty is augmented by it; as, on the other hand, beauty, which by itself produces pleasure, makes us receive

The direct survey of another’s pleasure naturally gives us pleasure, and therefore produces pain when compared with our own. His pain, considered in itself, is painful to us, but augments the idea of our own happiness, and gives us pleasure.

NOR will it appear strange that we may feel a reversed sensation from the happiness and misery of others; since we find the same comparison may give us a kind of malice against ourselves, and make us rejoice for our pains, and grieve for our pleasures. Thus the prospect of past pain is agreeable, when we are satisfied with our present condition; as on the other hand our past pleasures give us uneasiness, when we enjoy nothing at present equal to them. The comparison being the same, as when we reflect on the sentiments of others, must be attended with the same effects.

NAY a person may extend this malice against himself, even to his present fortune, and carry it so far as designedly to seek affliction, and increase his pains and sorrows.

*Of malice and envy.*

This may happen on two occasions. *First*, upon the distress and misfortune of a friend, or a person dear to him. *Secondly*, upon feeling any remorse for a crime of which he has been guilty. It is from the principle of comparison that both these irregular appetites for evil arise. A person who indulges himself in any pleasure while his friend suffers under affliction feels the reflected uneasiness from his friend more intensely by a comparison with the original pleasure which he himself enjoys. This contrast, indeed, ought also to enliven the present pleasure. But as grief is here supposed to be the predominant passion, every addition falls to that side and is swallowed up in it, without operating in the least upon the contrary affection. It is the same case with those penances which men inflict on themselves for their past sins and failings. When a criminal reflects on the punishment he deserves, the idea of it is magnified by a comparison with his present ease and satisfaction; which forces him, in a manner, to seek uneasiness in order to avoid such a disagreeable contrast.

This reasoning will account for the origin of *envy* as well as of malice. The only

The difference between these passions lies in this: envy is excited by the present enjoyment of another, which by comparison diminishes our idea of our own; whereas malice is the unprovoked desire of producing evil for another in order to gain pleasure from the comparison. The enjoyment which is the object of envy is commonly superior to our own. Superiority naturally seems to overshadow us and presents a disagreeable comparison. But even in the case of someone's inferiority, we still desire a greater distance in order to increase even more the idea of ourselves. When this distance diminishes, the comparison is less to our advantage; consequently, it gives us less pleasure and is even disagreeable. From this arises 그hat species of envy which people feel when they perceive their inferiors approaching or overtaking them in the pursuit of glory or happiness. In this envy, we may see the effects of comparison repeated twice. A man who compares himself to his inferior receives pleasure from the comparison; and when that inferiority decreases by the elevation of the inferior, what should have only been a decrease of pleasure becomes a real pain through a new comparison with the preceding condition.

<meta>catchword: 'Tis</meta>

<summary>Hume distinguishes envy, caused by another's superior enjoyment, from malice, the desire to harm another to gain a favorable comparison. He also explains how envy can arise when an inferior person improves their status, as the loss of a favorable comparison produces actual pain.</summary>

<margin>
SECTION VIII.
Of malice and envy.
</margin>

It is worth observing, concerning that envy which arises from the superiority of others, that it is not a great disproportion between ourselves and another that produces it; but on the contrary, our proximity. A common soldier feels no such envy toward his general as he does toward his sergeant or corporal; nor does a distinguished writer meet with as much jealousy from common hackneyed scribblers as from authors who more closely approach his level. It may, indeed, be thought that the greater the disproportion is, the greater the discomfort from the comparison must be. But we may consider, on the other hand, that a great disproportion cuts off the relationship, and either keeps us from comparing ourselves with what is remote from us or diminishes the effects of the comparison. Resemblance and proximity always produce a relationship of ideas; and where you destroy these ties, regardless of what other accidents may bring two ideas together—as they have no bond or connecting quality to join them in the imagination—it is impossible they can remain united for long or have any significant influence on each other.

I HAVE observed, in considering the nature of ambition, that the great feel a double

When the imagination, in the comparison of objects, does not pass easily from one object to the other, the action of the mind is, to a great extent, broken, and the imagination, in considering the second object, begins, as it were, on a new footing. The impression that accompanies every object does not seem greater in that case by following a smaller one of the same kind; instead, these two impressions are distinct and produce their distinct effects without any connection to each other. The lack of relation between the ideas breaks the relation between the impressions, and by such a separation prevents their mutual operation and influence.

To confirm this, we may observe that proximity in the degree of merit is not alone sufficient to give rise to envy but must be assisted by other relations. A poet is not likely to envy a philosopher, or a poet of a different kind, of a different nation, or of a different age. All these differences prevent or weaken the comparison and, consequently, the passion.

This is also the reason why all objects appear large or small merely by a comparison with those of the same species. From the same principle, we may account for the observation of historians that any party in a civil war always chooses to call in a foreign enemy at any risk rather than submit to their fellow citizens. *Guicciardin* <note>Francesco Guicciardini (1483–1540), an Italian historian and statesman.</note> applies this remark to the wars in *Italy*, where the relations between the different states are, properly speaking, nothing but those of name, language, and <term>contiguity</term>. THESE examples from history and common experience are rich and curious; but we may find parallel ones in the arts, which are no less remarkable. Should an author compose a treatise of which one part was serious and profound, and another light and humorous, everyone would condemn so strange a mixture, and would accuse him of neglecting all rules of art and criticism. Even supposing the reader should peruse these two compositions without any interval, he would feel little or no difficulty in the change of passions: Why, but because he considers these performances as entirely different, and by this break in the ideas, breaks the progress of the emotions, and hinders the one from influencing or contradicting the other?

**A** heroic and burlesque design, united in one picture, would be monstrous; though we place two pictures of such opposite character in the same chamber, and even close by each other, without any scruple or difficulty.

**I**n a word, no ideas can affect each other, either by comparison, or by the passions they separately produce, unless they are united together by some relation, which may cause an easy transition of the ideas, and consequently of the emotions or impressions attending the ideas; and may preserve the one impression in the passage of the imagination to the object of the other. This principle is very remarkable, because it is

### PART II.

analogous to what we have observed both concerning the *understanding* and the *passions*. Suppose two objects are presented to me, which are not connected by any kind of relation. Suppose that each of these objects separately produces a passion; and that these two passions are in themselves contrary: We find from experience that the lack of relation in the objects or ideas hinders the natural opposition of the passions, and that the break in the transition of the thought removes the affections from each other, and prevents their opposition. It is the same case with comparison; and from both these phenomena, we may safely conclude that the relation of ideas must promote the transition of impressions, since its absence alone is able to prevent it, and to separate what naturally should have operated upon each other. When the absence of an object or quality removes any usual or natural effect, we may certainly conclude that its presence contributes to the production of the effect.

<summary>Hume argues that for two passions to oppose or influence each other, the objects causing them must be linked by a relation of ideas. If no such relation exists, the mind’s transition is broken, allowing contrary emotions to exist separately without conflict.</summary>

## S E C T. IX.

### *Of the mixture of benevolence and anger with compassion and malice.*

<margin>Section IX.</margin>
Thus we have endeavored to account for *pity* and *malice*. Both these affections arise from the imagination, according to the light in which it places its object. When our fancy directly considers the sentiments of others and enters deep into them, it makes us sensible of all the passions it surveys, but in a particular manner of grief or sorrow. On the contrary, when we compare the sentiments of others to our own, we feel a sensation directly opposite to the original one, namely, a joy from the grief of others and a grief from their joy. But these are only the first foundations of the affections of pity and malice. Other passions are afterward mixed with them. There is always a mixture of love or tenderness with pity, and of hatred or anger with malice. But it must be confessed that this mixture seems at first sight to be contradictory to my system. For as

<summary>Hume explains that pity and malice arise from the imagination either through direct empathy or through a comparison with one's own state. He also notes that pity is typically mixed with love, while malice is mixed with hatred.</summary>

<keywords>pity, malice, imagination, sentiments, passion, benevolence, anger, David Hume, sympathy, comparison</keywords>

pity is an uneasiness, and malice a joy, arising from the misery of others, pity should naturally, as in all other cases, produce hatred; and malice, love. This contradiction I endeavor to reconcile in the following manner.

In order to cause a transition of passions, there is required a double relation of impressions and ideas, nor is one relation sufficient to produce this effect. But so that we may understand the full force of this double relation, we must consider that it is not the present sensation alone, or momentary pain or pleasure, which determines the character of any passion, but the whole bent or tendency of it from the beginning to the end. One impression may be related to another, not only when their sensations are similar, as we have all along supposed in the preceding cases; but also when their impulses or directions are similar and correspondent. This cannot take place with regard to pride and humility; because these are only pure sensations, without any direction or tendency toward action. We are, therefore, to look for instances of this peculiar relation of impressions only in such affections as are attended with a certain appe-

tite or desire; such as those of love and hatred.

<margin>Sect. IX. *Of the mixture of benevolence, etc.*</margin>
**Benevolence**, or the appetite which accompanies love, is a desire for the happiness of the person beloved and an aversion to his misery; just as anger, or the appetite which accompanies hatred, is a desire for the misery of the person hated and an aversion to his happiness. A desire, therefore, for the happiness of another and an aversion to his misery are similar to benevolence; and a desire for his misery and an aversion to his happiness correspond to anger. Now, pity is a desire for the happiness of another and an aversion to his misery, just as malice is the contrary appetite. Pity, then, is related to benevolence, and malice to anger. And as benevolence has already been found to be connected with love by a natural and original quality, and anger with hatred, it is by this chain that the passions of pity and malice are connected with love and hatred.

This hypothesis is founded on sufficient experience. A man who, from any motives, has entertained a resolution to perform an action, naturally runs into every other view or motive which may fortify that resolution and give it authority and influence

<summary>Hume explains that pity is related to benevolence and malice to anger because they share the same desires (appetites) regarding another person's happiness or misery. These shared impulses create the psychological chain that links pity to love and malice to hatred.</summary>

We may add to this another experiment, *namely*, that benevolence and anger, and consequently love and hatred, arise when our happiness or misery have any dependence on the happiness or misery of another person, without any further relation. I do not doubt that this experiment will appear so singular as to excuse us for stopping a moment to consider it.

Suppose that two persons of the same trade should seek employment in a town that is not able to maintain both; it is plain the success of one is perfectly incompatible with that of the other, and that whatever is for the interest of either is contrary to that of his rival, and so *vice versa*. Now it is evident that in the first case, hatred always follows from the opposition of interests; just as in the second, love arises from their union. Let us consider to what principle we can ascribe these passions.

It is plain they do not arise from the double relations of impressions and ideas, if we consider only the present sensation. For taking the first case of rivalry; although the pleasure and advantage of an antagonist necessarily causes my pain and loss, yet to counterbalance this, his pain and loss causes my pleasure and advantage; and supposing him to be unsuccessful, I may by this means receive from him a greater degree of satisfaction. In the same manner the success of a partner rejoices me, but then his misfortunes afflict me in an equal proportion; and it is easy to imagine that the latter sentiment may in many cases outweigh the former. But whether the fortune of a rival or partner be good or bad, I always hate the former and love the latter.

In order, then, to make a passion run parallel with benevolence, it is necessary that we should feel these double impressions, corresponding to those of the person whom we consider; nor is any one of them alone sufficient for that purpose. When we sympathize only with one impression, and that a painful one, this sympathy is related to anger and to hatred, on account of the uneasiness it conveys to us. But as the extensive or limited sympathy depends upon the force of the first sympathy; it follows, that the passion of love or hatred depends upon the same principle. A strong impression, when communicated, gives a double tendency of the passions; which is related to benevolence and love by a similarity of direction; however painful the first impression might have been. A weak impression, that is painful, is related to anger and hatred by the resemblance of sensations. Benevolence, therefore, arises from a great degree of misery, or any degree strongly sympathized with: Hatred or contempt from a small degree, or one weakly sympathized with; which is the principle I intended to prove and explain.

NOR do we have only our reason to trust for this principle, but also experience. A certain degree of poverty produces contempt; but a degree beyond that causes compassion and goodwill. We may undervalue a peasant or servant; but when the misery of a beggar appears very great, or is painted in very vivid colors, we sympathize with him in his afflictions, and feel in our hearts evident touches of pity and benevolence. The same object causes contrary passions according to its different degrees. The passions, therefore, must depend upon principles that operate in such certain degrees, according to my hypothesis. The increase of the sympathy has evidently the same effect as the increase of the misery.

A BARREN or desolate country always seems ugly and disagreeable, and commonly inspires us with contempt for the inhabitants. **BUT** although the force of the impression generally produces pity and benevolence, it is certain that by being carried too far it ceases to have that effect. This, perhaps, may be worth our notice. When the uneasiness is either small in itself, or remote from us, it does not engage the imagination, nor is it able to convey an equal concern for the future and contingent good, as for the present and real evil. Upon its acquiring greater force, we become so interested in the concerns of the person, as to be sensible both of his good and bad fortune; and from that complete sympathy there arises pity and benevolence. But it will easily be imagined, that where the present evil strikes with more than ordinary force, it may entirely engage our attention, and prevent that double sympathy, mentioned above. Of the mixture of benevolence, etc.

BUT the instance which most clearly supports my hypothesis is that where, by a change of the objects, we separate the double sympathy even from a moderate degree of the passion; in which case we find that pity, instead of producing love and tenderness as usual, always gives rise to the contrary affection. When we observe a person in misfortunes, we are affected with pity and love; but the author of that misfortune becomes the object of our strongest hatred, and is the more detested in proportion to the degree of our compassion. Now, for what reason should the same passion of pity produce love to the person who suffers the misfortune, and hatred to the person who causes it; unless it be because in the latter case the author bears a relation only to the misfortune; whereas in considering the sufferer, we look at every side, and wish for his prosperity as well as being sensible of his affliction?

# PART II.

<margin>
~
*Of love and hatred.*
</margin>

I shall just observe, before I leave the present subject, that this phenomenon of the double sympathy, and its tendency to cause love, may contribute to the production of the kindness that we naturally feel toward our relatives and acquaintances. Custom and relationship make us enter deeply into the sentiments of others; and whatever fortune we assume accompanies them is made present to us by the imagination and operates as if it were originally our own. We rejoice in their pleasures and grieve for their sorrows merely from the force of sympathy. Nothing that concerns them is indifferent to us; and as this correspondence of sentiments is the natural companion of love, it readily produces that affection.

## SECTION X.

### Of respect and contempt.

<margin>SECTION X.</margin>
There now remains only the task of explaining the passions of *respect* and *contempt*, along with *amorous affection*, in order to understand all the passions that have any mixture of love or hatred. Let us begin with respect and contempt.

In considering the qualities and circumstances of others, we may either regard them as they really are in themselves, or we may make a comparison between them and our own qualities and circumstances, or we may combine these two methods of consideration. From the first point of view, the good qualities of others produce love; from the second, they produce humility; and from the third, they produce respect, which is a mixture of these two passions. In the same way, their bad qualities cause either hatred, pride, or contempt, depending on the light in which we view them.

That there is a mixture of pride in contempt, and of humility in respect, is, I think,

<summary>Hume introduces respect and contempt as complex passions derived from how we perceive others in relation to ourselves. He defines respect as a mixture of love and humility, while contempt is a mixture of hatred and pride.</summary>
<keywords>respect, contempt, passions, love, hatred, humility, pride, amorous affection, human nature, David Hume</keywords>

too evident, from their very feeling or appearance, to require any particular proof.

<margin>
**PART II.**
{
*Of love and hatred.*
</margin>
That this mixture arises from a tacit comparison of the person contemned or respected with ourselves is no less evident. The same man may cause either respect, love, or contempt by his condition and talents, depending on whether the person who considers him, from being his inferior, becomes his equal or superior. In changing the point of view, although the object may remain the same, its proportion to ourselves entirely alters; which is the cause of an alteration in the passions. These passions, therefore, arise from our observing the proportion; that is, from a comparison.

I have already observed that the mind has a much stronger propensity to pride than to humility, and have endeavored, from the principles of human nature, to assign a cause for this phenomenon. Whether my reasoning is received or not, the phenomenon is undisputed and appears in many instances. Among the rest, it is the reason why there is a much greater mixture of pride in contempt than of humility in respect, and why we are more elevated by the view of one below us than mortified by the presence of one above us. Contempt

### SECTION X.
---
Of respect and contempt.

BUT here it may reasonably be asked why this mixture takes place only in some cases and does not appear on every occasion. All those objects that cause love when placed on another person are the causes of pride when transferred to ourselves; and consequently, they ought to be causes of humility as well as love when they belong to others and are only compared to those that we ourselves possess. In the same way, every quality which, by being directly considered, produces hatred, ought always to give rise to pride by comparison; and by a mixture of these passions of hatred and pride, it ought to excite contempt or scorn. The difficulty, then, is why any objects ever cause pure love or hatred and do not always produce the mixed passions of respect and contempt.

*Of love and hatred.*

humility and hatred are similar in their sensations, and that the two former <note>Referencing love and pride from the previous page.</note> are always agreeable, and the two latter painful. But though this is universally true, it is observable that the two agreeable, as well as the two painful passions, have some differences and even contrarieties which distinguish them. Nothing invigorates and exalts the mind equally with pride and vanity; though at the same time, love or tenderness is rather found to weaken and enfeeble it. The same difference is observable between the uneasy passions. Anger and hatred bestow a new force on all our thoughts and actions; while humility and shame deject and discourage us. Of these qualities of the passions, it will be necessary to form a distinct idea. Let us remember that pride and hatred invigorate the soul; and love and humility enfeeble it.

FROM this it follows that, although the conformity between love and hatred <note>This likely intended to say "love and pride," as hatred is not an agreeable sensation.</note> in the agreeableness of their sensation makes them always be excited by the same objects, yet this other contrariety is the reason why they are excited in very different degrees. Genius and learning are *pleasant* and *magnificent* objects, and by both these circumstances

<meta>catchword: stances</meta>

<summary>Hume distinguishes between the passions based on whether they invigorate or enfeeble the mind, noting that pride and hatred provide force while love and humility cause weakness. This distinction explains why these passions, despite sharing similar causes, are felt in varying degrees.</summary>
<keywords>pride, vanity, love, hatred, humility, passions, mind, soul, genius, learning, David Hume</keywords>

Ignorance and simplicity are *disagreeable* and *mean*, which in the same manner gives them a double connection with humility, and a single one with hatred. We may, therefore, consider it as certain, that though the same object always produces love and pride, or humility and hatred, according to its different situations, yet it seldom produces either the two former or the two latter passions in the same proportion.

<margin>
SECTION X.
<image-desc>flourished horizontal line</image-desc>
*Of respect and contempt.*
</margin>

It is here we must seek for a solution of the difficulty mentioned above: why any object ever excites pure love or hatred, and does not always produce respect or contempt, by a mixture of humility or pride. No quality in another gives rise to humility by comparison, unless it would have produced pride by being placed in ourselves; and *vice versa*, no object excites pride by comparison, unless it would have produced humility by the direct survey. This is evident: objects always produce by *comparison* a sensation directly contrary to their *original* one. Suppose, therefore, an object to be presented, which is peculiarly fitted to produce love, but imperfectly to excite pride;

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<summary>Hume explains that while the same objects trigger related passions, they do so in varying proportions because of their specific qualities. He introduces the study of respect and contempt, noting that these arise from comparing others' qualities to our own.</summary>
<keywords>pride, vanity, love, humility, hatred, respect, contempt, comparison, passions, sensation</keywords>

*Of love and hatred.*

This object, belonging to another, gives rise directly to a great degree of love, but only to a small degree of humility by comparison; and consequently, that latter passion is scarcely felt in the mixture, nor is it able to convert the love into respect. This is the case with good nature, good humor, facility, generosity, beauty, and many other qualities. These have a peculiar aptitude to produce love in others, but not as great a tendency to excite pride in ourselves. For this reason, the view of them as belonging to another person produces pure love, with only a small mixture of humility and respect. It is easy to extend the same reasoning to the opposite passions.

Before we leave this subject, it may be helpful to account for a rather curious phenomenon: namely, why we commonly keep at a distance those whom we despise, and do not allow our inferiors to approach too near, even in physical place and situation. It has already been observed that almost every kind of idea is accompanied by some emotion—even the ideas of number and extension—and much more so those ideas of objects that are esteemed of importance in life and capture our attention. It is not with entire indifference that we can sur-

<margin>Section X. Of respect and contempt.</margin>

These two passions are contrary to each other; but in order to make this contrariety be felt, the objects must be somehow related; otherwise the affections are totally separate and distinct, and never encounter each other. The relation takes place whenever the persons become contiguous; which is a general reason why we are uneasy at seeing such disproportionate objects as a rich man and a poor one, or a nobleman and a porter, in that situation.

**This** uneasiness, which is common to every spectator, must be more sensible to the superior; and that because the near approach of the inferior is regarded as a piece of ill-breeding, and shows that he is not sensible of the disproportion, and is in no way affected by it. A sense of superiority in another breeds in all men an inclination to keep themselves at a distance from him, and determines them to redouble the marks of respect and reverence when they are obliged to approach him; and where they do not observe that conduct, it is proof they are not sensible of his superiority. From hence

<summary>Hume explains that respect and contempt are opposing passions that only interact when people of different status are in close proximity. He argues that we feel uneasy seeing people of vastly different social classes near one another because physical distance is a primary way of acknowledging and showing respect for superiority.</summary>

too, it follows that any great *difference* in the degrees of any quality is called a *distance* by a common metaphor; this, however trivial it may appear, is founded on natural principles of the imagination. A great difference inclines us to create a distance. The ideas of distance and difference are, therefore, connected together. Connected ideas are easily mistaken for each other; and this is generally the source of the metaphor, as we shall have occasion to observe later.

## SECTION XI.

### *Of the amorous passion, or love between the sexes.*

Thus hunger may often be considered as the primary inclination of the soul, and the desire of approaching food as the secondary one, since it is absolutely necessary for satisfying that appetite. If an object, therefore, by any separate qualities, inclines us to approach the food, it naturally increases our appetite; just as, on the contrary, whatever inclines us to keep our food at a distance is contradictory to hunger and diminishes our inclination toward it. Now it is plain that beauty has the first effect, and deformity the second. This is the reason why the former gives us a keener appetite for our food, and the latter is sufficient to disgust us with the most savory dish that cookery has invented. There is no force of reflection or penetration required. Everything is conducted by mechanisms and principles which are not peculiar to man, or any one species of animals. The conclusion from this is obviously in favor of the foregoing system.

LOVE in animals does not have for its only object animals of the same species, but extends itself further and includes almost every perceptible and thinking being. A dog naturally loves a man more than his own species, and very commonly meets with a return of affection.

As animals are only slightly susceptible to either the pleasures or pains of the imagination, they can judge of objects only by the perceptible good or evil which they produce, and from *that* they must regulate their affections toward them. Of the

LOVE in beasts is not caused so much by relation as in our species; and that is because their thoughts are not so active as to trace relations, except in very obvious instances. Yet it is easy to remark that, on some occasions, it has a considerable influence upon them. Thus acquaintance, which has the same effect as relation, always produces love in animals, either toward men or toward each other. For the same reason, any likeness among them is the source of affection. An ox confined to a park with horses will naturally join their company, if I may so speak, but always leaves it to enjoy that of his own species where he has the choice of both.

THE affection of parents to their young proceeds from a peculiar instinct in animals, as well as in our species.

I desire it may be observed, that by the *will*, I mean nothing but *the internal impression we feel and are conscious of, when we knowingly give rise to any new motion of our body, or new perception of our mind.* This impression, like the preceding ones of pride and humility, love and hatred, it is impossible to define, and needless to describe any further; for which reason we shall cut off all those definitions and distinctions with which philosophers are accustomed to perplex rather than clear up this question; and entering at first upon the subject, shall examine that long-disputed question concerning *liberty* and *necessity*, which occurs so naturally in treating of the will.

It is universally acknowledged that the operations of external bodies are necessary, and that in the communication of their motion, in their attraction, and mutual cohesion, there are not the least traces of indifference or liberty. Every object is determined by an absolute fate to a certain degree and direction of its motion, and can no more depart from that precise line in which

determination of the mind to pass from one object to its usual attendant, and infer the existence of one from that of the other. Here, then, are two particulars which we are to consider as essential to necessity: namely, the constant *union* and the *inference* of the mind; and wherever we discover these, we must acknowledge a necessity. As the actions of matter have no necessity but what is derived from these circumstances, and it is not by any insight into the essence of bodies that we discover their connection, the absence of this insight—while the union and inference remain—will never, in any case, remove the necessity. It is the observation of the union which produces the inference; for which reason it might be thought sufficient if we prove a constant union in the actions of the mind in order to establish the inference, along with the necessity of these actions. But so that I may give my reasoning greater force, I shall examine these particulars separately, and shall first prove from experience that our actions have a constant union with our motives, tempers, and circumstances, before I consider the inferences we draw from it.

<summary>Hume defines the two essential components of necessity as the constant union of objects and the mind's subsequent inference from one to the other. He proposes to demonstrate that human actions are necessary by first proving they are constantly united with specific motives and circumstances.</summary>
<keywords>necessity, constant union, inference, actions of the mind, actions of matter, motives, human nature, will</keywords>

To this end, a very slight and general view of the common course of human affairs will be sufficient. There is no perspective from which we can view them that does not confirm this principle. Whether we consider mankind according to the difference of sexes, ages, governments, conditions, or methods of education, the same uniformity and regular operation of natural principles are discernible. Similar causes still produce similar effects, in the same manner as in the mutual action of the elements and powers of nature.

There are different trees which regularly produce fruit whose flavors are different from each other; and this regularity will be admitted as an instance of necessity and causes in external bodies. But are the products of *Guienne* and of *Champagne* more regularly different than the sentiments, actions, and passions of the two sexes, of which the one is distinguished by its force and maturity, the other by its delicacy and softness?

We must certainly admit that the cohesion of the parts of matter arises from natural and necessary principles, whatever difficulty we may find in explaining them. For a similar reason, we must admit that human society is founded on similar principles; and our reasoning in the latter case is even better than in the former. This is because we not only observe that men *always* seek society, but we can also explain the principles upon which this universal inclination is founded. For is it more certain that two flat pieces of marble will stick together than that two young savages of different sexes will mate? The skin, pores, muscles, and nerves of a day-laborer are different from those of a man of quality: so are his sentiments, actions, and manners. The different stations of life influence the whole fabric, external and internal; and these different stations arise necessarily, because uniformly, from the necessary and uniform principles of human nature. Men cannot live without society, and cannot be associated without government. Government makes a distinction of property, and establishes the different ranks of men. This produces industry, trade, manufactures, lawsuits, war, leagues, alliances, voyages, travels, cities, fleets, ports, and all those other actions and objects, which cause such a diversity, and at the same time maintain such a uniformity in human life.

There is a general course of nature in human actions, as well as in the operations of the sun and the climate. There are also characters peculiar to different nations and particular persons, as well as those common to mankind. The knowledge of these characters is founded on the observation of a uniformity in the actions that flow from them; and this uniformity forms the very essence of necessity.

I CAN imagine only one way of eluding this argument, which is by denying that uniformity of human actions on which it is founded. As long as actions have a constant union and connection with the situation and disposition of the agent, however we may in words refuse to acknowledge the necessity, we really allow the thing. To this I reply, that in judging the actions of men we must proceed upon the same maxims as when we reason concerning external objects. When any phenomena are constantly and invariably conjoined together, they acquire such a connection in the imagination that it passes from one to the other without any doubt or hesitation. But below this, there are many inferior degrees of evidence and probability, nor does one single contrary experiment entirely destroy all our reasoning. The mind balances the contrary experiments, and by deducting the inferior from the superior, proceeds with that degree of assurance or evidence which remains. ### SECT. I.

---

<margin>Of liberty and necessity.</margin>
We must now show that as the *union* between motives and actions has the *same* constancy as that in any natural operations, so its influence on the understanding is also the same in *determining* us to infer the existence of one from that of another. If this is shown, there is no known circumstance that enters into the connection and production of the actions of matter that is not to be found in all the operations of the mind; and consequently, we cannot, without an obvious absurdity, attribute necessity to one and deny it to the other.

There is no philosopher whose judgment is so fixed to this fantastical system of liberty as not to acknowledge the force of *moral evidence* and, in both speculation and *practice*, proceed upon it as a reasonable foundation. Now moral evidence is nothing but a conclusion concerning the actions of men, derived from the consideration of their motives, temper, and situation. Thus, when we see certain characters or figures described upon paper, we infer that the person who produced them would affirm such facts as the death of *Cæsar*, the success of *Augustus*,

### SECT. I.

---

<margin>*Of liberty and necessity.*</margin>
ALL those objects, of which we call one the *cause* and the other the *effect*, considered in themselves, are as distinct and separate from each other as any two things in nature; nor can we ever, by the most accurate survey of them, infer the existence of one from that of the other. It is only from experience and the observation of their constant union that we are able to form this inference; and even after all, the inference is nothing but the effects of custom on the imagination. We must not be content here with saying that the idea of cause and effect arises from objects constantly united, but must affirm that it is the very same as the idea of these objects, and that the *necessary connection* is not discovered by a conclusion of the understanding, but is merely a perception of the mind. Wherever, therefore, we observe the same union, and wherever the union operates in the same manner upon belief and opinion, we have the idea of causes and necessity, though perhaps we may avoid using those expressions. Motion in one body in all past instances that have fallen under our observation is

AND indeed, when we consider how aptly *natural* and *moral* evidence cement together, and form only one chain of argument between them, we shall have no hesitation to allow that they are of the same nature, and derived from the same principles. A prisoner, who has neither money nor influence, discovers the impossibility of his escape as much from the obstinacy of the jailer as from the walls and bars with which he is surrounded; and in all attempts for his freedom, he chooses rather to work upon the stone and iron of the one, than upon the inflexible nature of the other. The same prisoner, when conducted to the scaffold, foresees his death as certainly from the constancy and fidelity of his guards as from the operation of the axe or wheel. A THIRD reason why the doctrine of liberty has generally been better received in the world than its antagonist proceeds from *religion*, which has been very unnecessarily involved in this question. There is no method of reasoning more common, and yet none more blamable, than to try to refute any hypothesis in philosophical debates by claiming it has dangerous consequences for religion and morality. When any opinion leads us into absurdities, it is certainly false; but it is not certain an opinion is false just because it has dangerous consequences. Such topics, therefore, ought to be entirely avoided, as they contribute nothing to the discovery of truth, but only serve to make an opponent's character seem hateful. I observe this in general, without claiming any advantage from it. I submit myself frankly to an examination of this kind and dare to affirm that the doctrine of necessity, according to my explanation of it, is not only innocent but even advantageous to religion and morality.

<summary>Hume critiques the common practice of dismissing philosophical arguments based on their supposed "dangerous consequences" to religion or morality. He argues that the doctrine of necessity is not only compatible with these values but actually supports them.</summary>

I define necessity in two ways, consistent with the two definitions of *cause*, of which it forms an essential part. I place it either in the constant union and conjunction of similar objects, or in the mind's inference from one to the other. Now, necessity in both these senses has been universally (though tacitly) acknowledged to belong to the human will—in the schools, in the pulpit, and in common life. No one has ever pretended to deny that we can draw inferences concerning human actions, and that those inferences are founded on the experienced union of similar actions with similar motives and circumstances. The only point on which anyone might differ from me is either that perhaps they will refuse to call this "necessity"—but as long as the meaning is understood, I hope the word can do no harm—or that they will maintain there is something else in the operations of matter. Now, whether that is true or not is of no consequence to religion, whatever importance it may have for natural philosophy. I may be mistaken in asserting that we have no idea of any other connection in the actions of bodies, and I shall be glad to be further instructed on that point. But I am certain that I ascribe nothing to the ac-

Therefore, let no one put a malicious interpretation on my words by saying simply that I assert the necessity of human actions and place them on the same footing as the operations of inanimate matter. I do not attribute to the will that unintelligible necessity which is supposed to exist in matter. Instead, I attribute to matter that understandable quality—call it necessity or not—which the most strict orthodoxy does or must allow belongs to the will. Therefore, I change nothing in the accepted systems regarding the will, but only regarding material objects.

Furthermore, I shall go even further and assert that this kind of necessity is so essential to religion and morality that without it, both would be completely overturned, and every other assumption is entirely destructive to all laws, both *divine* and *human*. It is indeed certain that as all human laws are founded on rewards and punishments, it is supposed as a fundamental principle that these motives have an influence on the mind and both produce good actions and prevent evil ones. We may give this influence whatever name we please; but

as it is usually conjoined with the action, common sense requires it should be esteemed a cause, and be looked upon as an instance of that necessity, which I would establish.

This reasoning is equally solid when applied to *divine laws*, so far as the deity is considered as a legislator, and is supposed to inflict punishment and bestow rewards with a design to produce obedience. But I also maintain, that even where he does not act in his magisterial capacity, but is regarded as the avenger of crimes merely on account of their odiousness and deformity, not only is it impossible, without the necessary connection of cause and effect in human actions, for punishments to be inflicted in a way compatible with justice and moral equity; but also that it could never enter into the thoughts of any reasonable being to inflict them. The constant and universal object of hatred or anger is a person or creature endowed with thought and consciousness; and when any criminal or injurious actions excite that passion, it is only by their relation to the person or connection with him. But according to the doctrine of liberty or chance, this connection is reduced to nothing, nor are men more accountable for those actions which

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that divine and human justice require the doctrine of necessity, as punishment and reward only make sense if there is a stable connection between a person’s consciousness and their actions. Without this causal link, the doctrine of liberty or chance would make it impossible to hold individuals accountable for their behavior.</summary>

Actions are by their very nature temporary and perishing; and where they do not proceed from some cause in the characters and disposition of the person who performed them, they do not fix themselves upon him, and can neither contribute to his honor, if good, nor infamy, if evil. The action itself may be blameworthy; it may be contrary to all the rules of morality and religion: But the person is not responsible for it; and as it proceeded from nothing in him that is durable or constant, and leaves nothing of that nature behind it, it is impossible he can, on its account, become the object of punishment or vengeance. According to the hypothesis of liberty, therefore, a man is as pure and untainted after having committed the most horrid crimes as at the first moment of his birth, nor is his character anyway concerned in his actions; since they are not derived from it, and the wickedness of the one can never be used as a proof of the depravity of the other. But men are so inconsistent with themselves that, although they often assert that necessity utterly destroys all merit and demerit toward either mankind or superior powers, they still continue to reason based on these very principles of necessity in all their judgments concerning this matter. Men are not blamed for evil actions that they perform ignorantly and accidentally, whatever their consequences may be. Why? It is because the causes of these actions are only momentary and end with the actions themselves. Men are blamed less for evil actions that they perform hastily and without premeditation than for those that proceed from thought and deliberation. For what reason? It is because a hasty temper, though a constant cause in the mind, operates only at intervals and does not infect the whole character. Again, repentance wipes away every crime, especially if accompanied by an evident reformation of life and manners. How is this to be explained? It is by asserting that actions render a person criminal only insofar as they are proofs of criminal passions or principles in the mind; and when, by any al-

HERE, then, I turn to my adversary and desire him to free his own system from these odious consequences before he charges them upon others. Or if he rather chooses that this question should be decided by fair arguments before philosophers than by declamations before the people, let him return to what I have advanced to prove that liberty and chance are synonymous, and concerning the nature of moral evidence and the regularity of human actions. Upon a review of these reasonings, I cannot doubt of an entire victory; and therefore, having proved that all actions of the will have particular causes, I proceed to explain what these causes are and how they operate.

### S E C T.  III.

## *Of the influencing motives of the will.*

<margin>
SECT. III.
~~~
</margin>

NOTHING is more common in philosophy, and even in everyday life, than to talk of the struggle between passion and reason, to give the preference to reason, and to assert that men are only virtuous to the extent that they conform themselves to its dictates. Every rational creature, it is said, is obliged to regulate his actions by reason; and if any other motive or principle challenges the direction of his conduct, he ought to oppose it until it is entirely subdued, or at least brought into conformity with that superior principle. On this way of thinking, the greatest part of moral philosophy, ancient and modern, seems to be founded; nor is there a wider field, for both metaphysical arguments and popular speeches, than this supposed superiority of reason over passion. The eternity, consistency, and divine origin of the former have been displayed to the best advantage; the blindness, inconsistency, and deceitful-

The understanding exerts itself in two different ways, as it judges from demonstration or probability; as it regards the abstract relations of our ideas, or those relations of objects of which experience alone gives us information. I believe it will scarcely be asserted that the first species of reasoning alone is ever the cause of any action. As its proper province is the world of ideas, and as the will always places us in that of realities, demonstration and volition seem, on that account, to be totally removed from each other. Mathematics, indeed, is useful in all mechanical operations, and arithmetic in almost every art and profession; but it is not from themselves that they have any influence. Mechanics is the art of regulating the motions of bodies to *some designed end or purpose*; and the reason why we employ arithmetic in fixing the proportions of numbers is only so that we may discover

Abstract or demonstrative reasoning, therefore, never influences any of our actions, except as it directs our judgment concerning causes and effects; which leads us to the second operation of the understanding.

It is obvious that when we have the prospect of pain or pleasure from any object, we feel a consequent emotion of aversion or propensity, and are carried to avoid or embrace what will give us this uneasiness or satisfaction. It is also obvious that this emotion does not rest here, but making us cast our view on every side, comprehends whatever objects are connected with its original one by the relation of cause and effect. Here then reasoning takes place to discover this relation; and according as our reasoning varies, our actions receive a subsequent variation. It can never in the least concern us to know that such objects are causes, and such others effects, if both the causes and effects are indifferent to us. Where the objects themselves do not affect us, their connection can never give them any influence; and it is plain that, as reason is nothing but the discovery of this connection, it cannot be by its means that the objects are able to affect us.

Reason is, and ought only to be, the slave of the passions, and can never pretend to any other office than to serve and obey them. As this opinion may appear somewhat extraordinary, it may not be improper to confirm it by some other considerations.

A PASSION is an original existence, or, if you will, a modification of existence, and contains not any representative quality which renders it a copy of any other existence or modification. When I am angry, I am actually possessed with the passion, and in that emotion have no more a reference to any other object than when I am thirsty, or sick, or more than five feet high.     WHAT may at first occur on this head is that, as nothing can be contrary to truth or reason except what has a reference to it, and as only the judgments of our understanding have this reference, it must follow that passions can be contrary to reason only so far as they are *accompanied* with some judgment or opinion. According to this principle, which is so obvious and natural, it is only in two senses that any affection can be called unreasonable. First, when a passion—such as hope or fear, grief or joy, despair or security—is founded on the supposition of the existence of objects which really do not exist. Second, when in exerting any passion in action, we choose means insufficient for the designed end and deceive ourselves in our judgment of causes and effects. Where a passion is neither founded on false suppositions nor chooses means insufficient for the end, the understanding can neither justify nor condemn it. It is as little contrary to reason to prefer even my own acknowledged lesser good to my greater, and to have a more ardent affection for the former than the latter. A trivial good may, from certain circumstances, produce a desire superior to what arises from the greatest and most valuable enjoyment; nor is there anything more extraordinary in this than in mechanics to see one pound weight raise up a hundred by the advantage of its situation. In short, a passion must be accompanied by some false judgment in order for it to be unreasonable; and even then, it is not the passion, properly speaking, which is unreasonable, but the judgment.

THE consequences are evident. Since a passion can never, in any sense, be called unreasonable, except when founded on a false supposition, or when it chooses means insufficient for the designed end, it is impossible that reason and passion can ever oppose each other, or dispute for the government of the will and actions. I may will the performance of certain actions as a means of obtaining any desired good; but as my willing of these actions is only secondary, and founded on the supposition that they are causes of the proposed effect, as soon as I discover the falsehood of that supposition, they must become indifferent to me.

It is natural for one who does not examine objects with a strict philosophic eye to imagine that those actions of the mind are entirely the same which produce no different sensation and are not immediately distinguishable by feeling and perception. Reason, for instance, exerts itself without producing any sensible emotion; and except in the more sublime investigations of philosophy, or in the frivolous subtleties of the academic schools, it scarcely ever conveys any pleasure or unease. From this, it follows that every action of the mind which operates with the same calmness and tranquility is mistaken for reason by all those who judge things from their first view and appearance.

Now it is certain, there are certain calm desires and tendencies, which, though they are real passions, produce little emotion in the mind, and are known more by their effects than by the immediate feeling or sensation. These desires are of two kinds: either certain instincts originally implanted in our natures, such as benevolence and resentment, the love of life, and kindness to children; or the general appetite for good, and aversion to evil, considered merely as such. When any of these passions are calm, and cause no disorder in the soul, they are very readily taken for the determinations of reason, and are supposed to proceed from the same faculty as that which judges truth and falsehood. Their nature and principles have been supposed to be the same, because their sensations are not evidently different.

**B**ESIDE these calm passions, which often determine the will, there are certain violent emotions of the same kind, which likewise have a great influence on that faculty. When I receive any injury from another, I often feel a violent passion of resentment, which makes me desire his evil and punishment, independent of all considerations

The common error of metaphysicians has been in ascribing the direction of the will entirely to one of these principles, and supposing the other to have no influence. People often act knowingly against their interest; for which reason the view of the greatest possible good does not always influence them. People often counteract a violent passion in pursuit of their interests and designs; it is not, therefore, the present uneasiness alone which determines them. In general, we may observe that both these principles operate on the will; and where they are contrary, either of them prevails according to the *general character* or *present disposition* of the person. What we call strength of mind implies the prevalence of the calm passions over the violent; though we may easily observe there is no man so constantly possessed of this virtue as to never, on any occasion, yield to the solicitations of passion and desire. From these variations of temper proceeds the great difficulty of

## SECT. IV.

# *Of the causes of the violent passions.*

<margin>
**SECT. IV.**
---
</margin>
There is not in philosophy a subject of more subtle speculation than this of the different *causes* and *effects* of the calm and violent passions. It is evident that passions do not influence the will in proportion to their violence, or the disorder they occasion in the temperament; but on the contrary, once a passion has become a settled principle of action and is the predominant inclination of the soul, it commonly no longer produces any noticeable agitation. As repeated custom and its own force have made everything yield to it, it directs actions and conduct without that opposition and emotion which so naturally attend every momentary gust of passion. We must, therefore, distinguish between a calm and a weak passion, and between a violent and a strong one. But notwithstanding this,

<vocab>philosophy, causes and effects, passions, will, principle of action, calm passion, violent passion</vocab>

<summary>Hume argues that the strength of a passion is not always indicated by its outward violence; settled passions often direct the will calmly because they have become established principles of action. He emphasizes the need to distinguish between a passion's strength and its emotional intensity.</summary>
<keywords>Hume, passions, will, calm passions, violent passions, principle of action, temperament, philosophy, human nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues David Hume’s exploration of the causes behind violent and calm passions, specifically focusing on how the proximity or situation of an object influences the intensity of the passion and its effect on the will.</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>

<margin>
SECT. IV.
Of the causes of the violent passions.
</margin>
It is certain that when we would govern a man and push him toward any action, it will commonly be better policy to work upon the violent rather than the calm passions, and rather take him by his inclination than by what is vulgarly called his *reason*. We ought to place the object in such particular situations as are proper to increase the violence of the passion. For we may observe that all depends upon the situation of the object, and that a variation in this particular will be able to change the calm and the violent passions into each other. Both these kinds of passions pursue good and avoid evil; and both of them are increased or diminished by the increase or diminution of the good or evil. But herein lies the difference between them: The same good, when near, will cause a violent passion, which, when remote, produces only a calm one. As this subject belongs very properly to the present question concerning the will, we shall examine it thoroughly here and shall consider some of those circumstances and situations of objects which render a passion either calm or violent.

It is a remarkable property of human nature that any emotion which accompanies a passion,

It is a common trick of politicians, when they want to affect any person deeply by a fact of which they intend to inform him, first to excite his curiosity; to delay satisfying it as long as possible; and by that means raise his anxiety and impatience to the utmost before they give him a full insight into the business. They know that his curiosity will drive him into the passion they design to raise and assist the object in its influence on the mind. A soldier advancing to battle is naturally inspired with courage and confidence when he thinks of his friends and fellow soldiers, and is struck with fear and terror when he reflects on the enemy. Whatever new emotion, therefore, arises from the former naturally increases the courage; just as the same emotion, arising from the latter, increases the fear, by the association of ideas and the conversion of the inferior emotion into the predominant one. This is why in military discipline, the uniformity and luster of our uniforms, the regularity of our formations

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>Hume explains how politicians and military leaders use secondary emotions like curiosity and anxiety to strengthen a person's primary passion. He argues that any emotion arising from a related idea will eventually be converted into the dominant passion, such as a soldier's confidence being bolstered by the sight of his comrades.</summary>

Since passions, however independent, are naturally transfused into each other if they are both present at the same time, it follows that when good or evil is placed in such a situation as to cause any particular emotion besides its direct passion of desire or aversion, that latter passion must acquire new force and violence.

This happens, among other cases, whenever any object excites contrary passions. For it is observable that an opposition of passions commonly causes a new emotion in the spirits and produces more disorder than the concurrence of any two affections of equal force. This new emotion is easily converted into the predominant passion and increases its violence beyond the level it would have reached had it met with no opposition. Hence we naturally desire what is forbidden and take a pleasure in performing actions merely because they are unlawful. The notion of duty, when opposite to the passions, is seldom able to overcome them;

<summary>Hume explains that when multiple emotions occur simultaneously, they tend to merge, with the resulting energy reinforcing the dominant passion. This explains why opposition or prohibition can actually increase desire, as the emotional conflict fuels the prevailing impulse.</summary>

### SECT. III.

<margin>Of the causes of violent passions.</margin>
THE same effect follows whether the opposition arises from internal motives or external obstacles. The passion commonly acquires new force and violence in both cases. The efforts the mind makes to surmount the obstacle excite the spirits and enliven the passion.

UNCERTAINTY has the same influence as opposition. The agitation of thought, the quick turns it makes from one view to another, and the variety of passions that succeed each other according to these different views: all these produce an agitation in the mind and transfuse themselves into the predominant passion.

THERE is not, in my opinion, any other natural cause why security diminishes the passions than because it removes that uncertainty which increases them. The mind, when left to itself, immediately languishes; and in order to preserve its ardor, it must be supported every moment by a new flow of passion. For the same reason, despair, though contrary to security, has a similar influence.

<summary>Hume argues that both external obstacles and internal uncertainty increase the intensity of a passion by creating mental agitation that feeds into the dominant emotion. Conversely, a sense of security diminishes passion by removing this necessary stimulation, much as despair can also dampen emotional energy.</summary>
<keywords>passions, uncertainty, security, despair, motives, principles, obstacles, agitation, predominant passion, animal spirits</keywords>

<page-num>260</page-num>

*Of the*

It is certain that nothing more powerfully stimulates any affection than to conceal some part of its objects by casting it into a kind of shade; while this shows enough to pre-possess us in favor of the object, it still leaves some work for the imagination. Furthermore, because obscurity is always accompanied by a kind of uncertainty, the effort the imagination makes to complete the idea rouses the spirits and gives additional force to the passion.

Just as despair and security, though contrary to each other, produce the same effects, so absence is observed to have contrary effects, and in different circumstances either increases or diminishes our affections. The *Duke de la Rochefoucauld* has very well observed that absence destroys weak passions but increases strong ones, just as the wind blows out a candle but fans a fire. Long absence naturally weakens our idea and diminishes the passion; but where the idea is so strong and vivid as to support itself, the uneasiness arising from absence increases the passion and gives it new force and intensity.

<meta>catchword: SECT.</meta>

<summary>Hume explains how obscurity and uncertainty heighten passion by stimulating the imagination. He also illustrates how absence weakens faint emotions but intensifies strong ones, comparing the effect to how wind affects a candle versus a fire.</summary>

## S E C T. V.

### *Of the effects of custom.*

<margin>S E C T. V. }</margin>BUT nothing has a greater effect both to increase and diminish our passions, to convert pleasure into pain, and pain into pleasure, than custom and repetition. Custom has two *original* effects upon the mind, in bestowing a *facility* in the performance of any action or the conception of any object; and afterwards a *tendency* or *inclination* towards it; and from these we may account for all its other effects, however extraordinary.

When the soul applies itself to the performance of any action, or the conception of any object, to which it is not accustomed, there is a certain unpliableness in the faculties, and a difficulty of the spirits' moving in their new direction. As this difficulty excites the spirits, it is the source of wonder, surprise, and of all the emotions, which arise from novelty; and is in itself very agreeable, like everything, which enlivens the mind to a moderate degree. But

Though surprise is agreeable in itself, yet as it puts the spirits in agitation, it not only increases our agreeable affections, but also our painful ones, according to the previous principle *that every emotion which precedes or attends a passion is easily converted into it.* Hence, everything that is new is most affecting and gives us either more pleasure or pain than what, strictly speaking, naturally belongs to it. When it often returns to us, the novelty wears off; the passions subside; the hurry of the spirits is over; and we survey the objects with greater tranquility.

By degrees, repetition produces a facility, which is another very powerful principle of the human mind and an infallible source of pleasure, where the facility does not go beyond a certain degree. And here it is remarkable that the pleasure which arises from a moderate facility does not have the same tendency as that which arises from novelty to increase the painful as well as the agreeable affections. The pleasure of facility does not so much consist in any ferment of the spirits as in their orderly motion, which will sometimes be so powerful as even to convert pain into pleasure, and give
