BUT again, as facility converts pain into pleasure, so it often converts pleasure into pain, when it is too great, and renders the actions of the mind so faint and languid, that they are no longer able to interest and support it. And indeed, scarcely any other objects become disagreeable through custom; except those that are naturally attended with some emotion or affection, which is destroyed by too frequent repetition. One can consider the clouds, and heavens, and trees, and stones, however frequently repeated, without ever feeling any aversion. But when the fair sex, or music, or good cheer, or anything that naturally ought to be agreeable, becomes indifferent, it easily produces the opposite affection.

BUT custom not only gives a facility to perform any action, but likewise an inclination and tendency toward it, where it is not entirely disagreeable, and can never be the object of inclination. And this is the reason why custom increases all *active* habits, but diminishes *passive* ones, according to the observation of a late eminent philosopher <note>Likely a reference to Joseph Butler, who discussed active and passive habits in 'The Analogy of Religion' (1736).</note>. ### S E C T. VI.

#### *Of the influence of the imagination on the passions.*

<margin>
SECT. VI.
</margin>
IT is remarkable that the imagination and affections have a close union with each other, and that nothing which affects the former can be entirely indifferent to the latter. Wherever our ideas of good or evil acquire a new vivacity, the passions become more violent and keep pace with the imagination in all its variations. Whether this proceeds from the principle mentioned above—*that any attendant emotion is easily converted into the predominant one*—I shall not determine. It is sufficient for my present purpose that we have many instances to confirm this influence of the imagination upon the passions.

~~~
Of the influence of the imagination, etc.
</margin>
Any pleasure with which we are acquainted affects us more than any other which we acknowledge to be superior, but of whose nature we are wholly ignorant. Of the one, we can form a particular and determinate idea; the other we conceive under the general notion of pleasure. It is certain that the more general and universal any of our ideas are, the less influence they have upon the imagination. A general idea, though it is nothing but a particular one considered in a certain view, is commonly more obscure. This is because no particular idea by which we represent a general one is ever fixed or determinate, but may easily be changed for other particular ones that will serve equally in the representation.

There is a noted passage in the history of *Greece* that may serve for our present purpose. *Themistocles* told the *Athenians* that he had formed a design which would be highly useful to the public, but which it was impossible for him to communicate to them without ruining its execution, since its success depended entirely on the secrecy with which it should be conducted. The *Athenians*, instead of granting him full power to act as he thought fitting, ordered

For my part, I see nothing so extraordinary in this proceeding of the *Athenians*. The same reasons which make it so easy for philosophers to establish these sublime maxims tend, in part, to diminish the merit of such conduct in that people. Philosophers never balance between profit and honesty because their decisions are general, and neither their passions nor their imaginations are interested in the objects. And although in the present case the advantage was immediate to the *Athenians*, yet as it was known only under the *general* notion of advantage, without being conceived by any particular idea, it must have had a less considerable influence on their imaginations, and have been a less violent temptation, than if they had been acquainted with all its circumstances: Otherwise, it is difficult to conceive that a whole people, unjust and violent as men commonly are, should so unanimously have adhered to justice and rejected any considerable advantage.

### S E C T. VII.

### *Of contiguity and distance in space and time.*

<margin>
S E C T.
VII.
~~~
</margin>

THERE is a simple reason why everything contiguous to us, either in space or time, should be conceived with a peculiar force and vivacity, and excel every other object in its influence on the imagination. Our self is intimately present to us, and whatever is related to the self must partake of that quality. But why, when an object is so far removed as to have lost the advantage of this relation, its idea becomes even fainter and more obscure as it is further removed, would perhaps require a more detailed examination.

It is obvious that the imagination can never totally forget the points of space and time in which we exist; but it receives such frequent reminders of them from the passions and senses that, however it may turn its attention to foreign and remote objects, it is forced every moment to re-

It is also remarkable that in the conception of those objects which we regard as real and existent, we take them in their proper order and situation, and never leap from one object to another that is distant from it without running over, at least in a cursory manner, all those objects that are interposed between them. When we reflect, therefore, on any object distant from ourselves, we are obliged not only to reach it at first by passing through all the intermediate space between ourselves and the object, but also to renew our progress every moment; being every moment recalled to the consideration of ourselves and our present situation. It is easily conceived that this interruption must weaken the idea by breaking the action of the mind, and hindering the conception from being as intense and continuous as when we reflect on a nearer object. The *fewer* steps we make to arrive at the object, and the *smoother* the road is, this diminution of vivacity is less <gloss>noticeably</gloss> considerably felt, but it may still be observed more or less in proportion to the degrees of distance and difficulty.

This is their effect on the imagination. If my reasoning is correct, they must have a proportional effect on the will and passions. Contiguous objects must have an influence much superior to the distant and remote. Accordingly, we find in common life that men are principally concerned about those objects which are not much removed either in space or time, enjoying the present and leaving what is far off to the care of chance and fortune. Talk to a man of his condition thirty years from now, and he will not regard you. Speak of what is to happen tomorrow, and he will give you his attention. The breaking of a mirror gives us more concern when at home than the burning of a house when abroad and several hundred leagues distant.

Twenty years is certainly but a small distance of time in comparison to what history and even the memory of some may inform them of, and yet I doubt if a thousand leagues, or even the greatest distance of place this globe can admit of, will so remarkably weaken our ideas and diminish our passions. A *West-India* merchant will tell you that he is not without concern about what passes in *Jamaica*; though few extend their views so far into the future as to dread very remote accidents.

The cause of this phenomenon must evidently lie in the different properties of space and time. Without resorting to metaphysics, anyone may easily observe that space or extension consists of a number of co-existent parts disposed in a certain order, and capable of being present at once to the sight or feeling. On the contrary, time or succession, though it also consists of parts, never presents to us more than one at once; nor is it possible for any two of them ever to be co-existent. These qualities of the objects have a corresponding effect on the imagination. The parts of extension

On the other hand, the incompatibility of the parts of time in their real existence separates them in the imagination and makes it more *difficult* for that faculty to trace any long succession or series of events. Every part must appear single and alone, nor can it normally enter the imagination without banishing what is supposed to have been immediately preceding. By this means, any distance in time causes a *greater* interruption in thought than an *equal* distance in space, and consequently weakens the idea more considerably—and consequently the passions, which depend in large part on the imagination, according to my system.

THERE is another phenomenon of a similar nature to the previous one, namely, *the superior effects of the same distance in the future compared to that in the past*. <margin>
SECTION VII.
<image-desc>decorative horizontal flourish</image-desc>
*Of contiguity, and distance in space and time.*
</margin>
BESIDES the propensity for a gradual progression through points of space and time, we have another peculiarity in our method of thinking which concurs in producing this phenomenon. We always follow the succession of time in placing our ideas, and from the consideration of any object, we pass more easily to that which follows immediately after it than to that which went before it. We may learn this, among other instances, from the order which is always observed in historical narratives. Nothing but an absolute necessity can force a historian to break the order of time and, in his narrative, give precedence to an event which was in reality later than another.

THIS will easily be applied to the question at hand if we reflect on what I have previously observed: that the present situation of the person is always that of the imagination, and that it is from there that we proceed to the conception of any distant object. When the object is past, the progression of the thought in passing to it from the pre-

<meta>catchword: sent</meta>
<sig>T 2</sig>

<summary>Hume explains that the imagination naturally follows the chronological order of time, moving more easily from a cause to its effect than backward from an effect to its cause. He illustrates this through the structure of historical narratives and notes that our imagination always begins its journey from our present situation.</summary>
<keywords>contiguity, space and time, succession of time, historical narrations, imagination, chronological order, mental progression</keywords>

sent is contrary to nature, as it proceeds from one point of time to that which precedes it, and from that to another preceding it, in opposition to the natural course of succession. On the other hand, when we turn our thought to a future object, our fancy flows along the stream of time and arrives at the object by an order which seems most natural, passing always from one point of time to that which is immediately later than it. This *easy* progression of ideas favors the imagination and makes it conceive its object in a stronger and fuller light than when we are continually opposed in our passage and are obliged to overcome the difficulties arising from the natural propensity of the fancy. A small degree of distance in the past, therefore, has a greater effect in interrupting and weakening the conception than a much greater distance in the future. From this effect on the imagination is derived its influence on the will and passions.

There is another cause which both contributes to the same effect and proceeds from the same quality of the fancy, by which we are determined to trace the succession of time by a similar succession of ideas. When from the present instant we

For as the future will *sometime* be present, so the past was *once* present. If we could, therefore, remove this quality of the imagination, an equal distance in the past and in the future would have a similar influence. Nor is this only true when the fancy remains fixed and, from the present instant, surveys the future and the past; but also when it changes its situation and places us in different periods of time. For as on the one hand, in supposing ourselves to exist at a point of time interposed between the present instant and the future object, we find the future object approach to us and the past retire and become more distant; so on the other hand, in supposing ourselves to exist at a point of time interposed between the present and the past, the past approaches to us and the future becomes more distant. ## SECTION VIII.

### *The same subject continued.*

<margin>
SECTION
VIII.
<image-desc>horizontal flourish</image-desc>
</margin>
Thus we have accounted for three phenomena, which seem quite remarkable: Why distance weakens the conception and passion; why distance in time has a greater effect than distance in space; and why distance in past time has an even greater effect than distance in the future. We must now consider three phenomena, which seem to be, in a sense, the reverse of these: Why a very great distance increases our esteem and admiration for an object; why such a distance in time increases it more than distance in space; and why distance in past time increases it more than distance in the future. The curiosity of the subject will, I hope, excuse my dwelling on it for some time.

To begin with the first phenomenon, why a great distance increases our esteem and admiration for an object: it is evident that the mere view and contemplation of any greatness, whether successive <note>occurring in sequence through time</note> or extended <note>occupying space</note>, enlarges the soul, and gives it a sensible delight and pleasure. the admiration, which arises from that distance,
by another natural transition, returns
back to the object.

But though every great distance produces an admiration for the distant object, a distance in time has a more considerable effect than that in space. Ancient busts and inscriptions are more valued than *Japan* tables: And not to mention the *Greeks* and *Romans*, it is certain we regard with more veneration the old *Chaldeans* and *Egyptians*, than the modern *Chinese* and *Persians*, and bestow more fruitless pains to clear up the history and chronology of the former, than it would cost us to make a voyage, and be certainly informed of the character, learning and government of the latter. I shall be obliged to make a digression in order to explain this phenomenon.

It is a quality very observable in human nature, that any opposition, which does not entirely discourage and intimidate us, has rather a contrary effect, and inspires us with a more than ordinary grandeur and magnanimity. In collecting our force to overcome the opposition, we invigorate the soul, and give it an elevation with which otherwise it would never have been acquainted. Compliance, by rendering our strength useless, makes us insensible of it; but opposition awakens and employs it.

<note>From Virgil's Aeneid: "He prays that among these listless herds a foaming boar might be granted to his vows, or a tawny lion might descend from the mountain."</note>

Whatever supports and fulfills the passions is agreeable to us; conversely, whatever weakens and enfeebles them is uncomfortable. As opposition has the first effect, and ease has the second, it is no wonder the mind, in certain moods, desires the former and is averse to the latter.

These principles affect the imagination as well as the passions. To be convinced of this, we need only consider the influence of *heights* and *depths* on that faculty. Any great elevation of place communicates a kind of pride or sublimity of imagination, and gives a fancied superiority over those that lie below; and, *vice versa*, a sublime and strong imagination conveys the idea of ascent and elevation. It follows from this that we associate, in a way, the

A noble genius is called an elevated and sublime one. *Atque udam spernit humum fugiente penna.* <note>From Horace: "And [virtue] spurns the damp earth on a fleeing wing."</note> On the contrary, a vulgar and trivial conception is styled indifferently low or mean. These methods of thinking, and of expressing ourselves, are not of as little consequence as they may appear at first sight.

It is evident to common sense, as well as philosophy, that there is no natural nor essential difference between high and low, and that this distinction arises only from the gravitation of matter, which produces a motion from the one to the other. The very same direction, which in this part of the globe is called *ascent*, is termed *descent* in our antipodes; which can proceed from nothing but the contrary tendency of bodies. Virtue, genius, power, and riches are for this reason associated with height and sublimity; as poverty, slavery, and folly are conjoined with descent and lowness. If our situation were the same as *Milton* represents that of the angels—for whom *descent* is *adverse*, and who *cannot sink without labor and compulsion*—this order of things would be entirely inverted. This is evident because the very nature of ascent and descent is derived from difficulty and propensity, and consequently, every one of their effects proceeds from that origin.

### SECT. VIII.
<margin>*The same subject continued.*</margin>

We are not inclined to imagine that our posterity will excel us, or even equal our ancestors. This phenomenon is the more remarkable because any distance in the future does not weaken our ideas as much as an equal distance in the past. Although a distance in the past, when very great, increases our passions beyond a similar distance in the future, a small distance has a greater influence in diminishing them.

In our common way of thinking, we are placed in a kind of middle position between the past and future; and as our imagination finds a certain difficulty in running along the former, and an ease in following the course of the latter, the difficulty conveys the notion of ascent, and the ease conveys the contrary. Hence, we imagine our ancestors to be, in a manner, mounted above us, and our posterity to lie below us. Our imagination does not arrive at the one without effort, but easily reaches the other. This effort weakens the conception where the distance is small; but it enlarges and elevates the imagination when accompanied by a suitable object. On the other hand, the ease assists the imagination in a small distance, but

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that we perceive the past as an "ascent" requiring mental effort, which leads us to venerate our ancestors more than our posterity. He explains that while the imagination moves easily toward the future, the difficulty of looking back into the past serves to elevate our passions and sense of esteem.</summary>

### PART III.
<image-desc>typographic flourish</image-desc>

<margin>Concerning the will and direct passions.</margin>
Before we leave this subject of the will, it may be appropriate to summarize in a few words all that has been said about it, in order to present the whole more clearly to the reader. What we commonly understand by *passion* is a violent and noticeable emotion of the mind, occurring when any good or evil is presented, or any object that is suited to excite an appetite due to the original structure of our faculties. By *reason*, we mean affections of the very same kind as the former, but those that operate more calmly and cause no disorder in one's temperament. This tranquility leads us to make a mistake about them, causing us to view them only as conclusions of our intellectual faculties. Both the *causes* and *effects* of these violent and calm passions are quite variable and depend, to a great extent, on the unique temperament and disposition of each individual. Generally speaking, the violent passions have a more powerful influence on the will; though it is often found that the calm ones, when supported by reflection and backed by resolution, are able to control them in their most fu-
<meta>catchword: rious</meta>

<summary>Hume transitions to discussing the will and passions, defining "passion" as a violent emotion and "reason" as a calm affection often mistaken for a purely intellectual conclusion. He notes that while violent passions usually drive the will, calm passions can prevail when supported by reflection and resolution.</summary>
<keywords>will, passion, reason, temperament, appetite, violent passions, calm passions, David Hume, human nature</keywords>

What makes this whole affair more uncertain is that a calm passion may easily be changed into a violent one, either by a change of temperament or of the circumstances and situation of the object—such as by borrowing force from any attendant passion, by custom, or by exciting the imagination. On the whole, this struggle of passion and of reason, as it is called, diversifies human life and makes men so different not only from each other, but also from themselves at different times. Philosophy can only account for a few of the greater and more noticeable events of this war, but must leave all the smaller and more delicate revolutions as dependent on principles too fine and minute for its comprehension.

### S E C T. IX.

### *Of the direct passions.*

<margin>SECT. IX.</margin>
It is easy to observe that the passions, both direct and indirect, are founded on pain and pleasure, and that in order to produce an affection of any kind, it is only requisite to present some good or evil. Upon the removal of pain and pleasure there immediately follows a removal of love and hatred, pride and humility, desire and aversion, and of most of our reflective or secondary impressions.

The impressions which arise from good and evil most naturally, and with the least preparation, are the *direct* passions of desire and aversion, grief and joy, hope and fear, along with volition. The mind by an *original* instinct tends to unite itself with the good, and to avoid the evil, though they be conceived merely in idea, and be considered as existing in any future period of time.

But supposing that there is an immediate impression of pain or pleasure, and *that* arising from an object related to ourselves or others,

<summary>Hume introduces the direct passions—desire, aversion, grief, joy, hope, fear, and volition—explaining that they arise directly from the experience or idea of pain and pleasure. He asserts that the mind possesses an original instinct to pursue good and avoid evil, which serves as the foundation for these reflective impressions.</summary>
<keywords>direct passions, indirect passions, pain, pleasure, desire, aversion, grief, joy, hope, fear, volition, original instinct, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how objects related to ourselves or others trigger passions, specifically focusing on the interaction between direct and indirect passions.</meta>

<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>
<sig>U 2</sig>

**S E C T.**
**IX.**
---
*Of the direct passions.*
</margin>
this does not prevent the propensity or aversion, along with the resulting emotions, but by concurring with certain dormant principles of the human mind, it excites the new impressions of *pride* or *humility*, *love* or *hatred*. That propensity, which unites us to the object or separates us from it, still continues to operate, but in conjunction with the *indirect* passions, which arise from a double relation of impressions and ideas.

**These** indirect passions, being always agreeable or uneasy, give in their turn additional force to the direct passions, and increase our desire and aversion to the object. Thus a suit of fine clothes produces pleasure from its beauty; and this pleasure produces the direct passions, or the impressions of volition and desire. Again, when these clothes are considered as belonging to ourselves, the double relation conveys to us the sentiment of pride, which is an indirect passion; and the pleasure, which attends that passion, returns back to the direct affections, and gives new force to our desire or volition, joy or hope.

**When** good is certain or probable, it produces joy. When evil is in the same situation there arises **GRIEF or SORROW**.

<summary>Hume explains how indirect passions like pride and love reinforce direct passions by adding additional force to our desires or aversions. He also defines the basic origins of joy and grief based on the certainty or probability of good and evil.</summary>

</margin>

**WHEN** either good or evil is uncertain, it gives rise to **FEAR** or **HOPE**, according to the degrees of uncertainty on one side or the other.

**DESIRE** arises from good considered simply, and **AVERSION** is derived from evil. The **WILL** exerts itself when either the good or the absence of the evil may be attained by any action of the mind or body.

**BESIDE** good and evil—or in other words, pain and pleasure—the direct passions frequently arise from a natural impulse or instinct, which is perfectly unaccountable. Of this kind is the desire for the punishment of our enemies and for the happiness of our friends; hunger, lust, and a few other bodily appetites. These passions, properly speaking, produce good and evil, and do not proceed from them like the other affections.

**NONE** of the direct affections seem to merit our particular attention except hope and fear, which we shall here endeavor to account for. It is evident that the very same event which, by its certainty, would produce grief or joy, always gives rise to fear or hope when it is only probable and uncertain. In order, therefore, to understand

<summary>Hume defines the origins of hope, fear, desire, and aversion, noting that the will is activated by the pursuit of good or the avoidance of evil. He also distinguishes between passions derived from pleasure and pain and those arising from primary instincts like hunger or the desire for a friend's happiness.</summary>

To understand the reason why this circumstance makes such a significant difference, we must reflect on what I have already put forward in the preceding book concerning the nature of probability.

<margin>
SECTION IX.
---
*Of the direct passions.*
</margin>

**Probability** arises from an opposition of contrary chances or causes, by which the mind is not allowed to fix on either side, but is incessantly tossed from one to another, and at one moment is determined to consider an object as existing, and at another moment as the contrary. The imagination or understanding, call it which you please, fluctuates between the opposite views; and though perhaps it may be more often turned to one side than the other, it is impossible for it, because of the opposition of causes or chances, to rest on either. The *pro* and *con* of the question alternately prevail; and the mind, surveying the object in its opposite principles, finds such a contradiction that it utterly destroys all certainty and established opinion.

**Suppose**, then, that the object whose reality we doubt is an object of either desire or aversion; it is evident that, as the mind turns itself to one side or the other, it

An object whose existence we desire gives satisfaction when we reflect on those causes that produce it; and for the same reason, it excites grief or uneasiness from the opposite consideration. So, as the understanding in all probable questions is divided between contrary points of view, the affections must in the same manner be divided between opposite emotions.

Now if we consider the human mind, we shall find that, with regard to the passions, it is not of the nature of a wind instrument of music, which, in running over all the notes, immediately loses the sound after the breath ceases; but rather, it resembles a string instrument, where after each stroke the vibrations still retain some sound, which gradually and imperceptibly decays. The imagination is extremely quick and agile, but the passions are slow and stubborn. For this reason, when any object is presented that provides a variety of views to the one and emotions to the other, although the fancy may change its views with great speed, each stroke will not produce a clear and distinct note of passion; instead, one passion will always be mixed and confused with the other.

This results in a mixture of conflicting emotions when the mind considers probable outcomes.</summary>

As the probability leans toward good or evil, the passion of joy or sorrow predominates in the combination: Because the nature of probability is to direct a greater number of views or chances toward one side; or, what amounts to the same thing, a greater number of recurrences of one passion; or since the dispersed passions are gathered into one, a higher degree of that passion. That is, in other words, when grief and joy are intermingled with each other by means of the opposing views of the imagination, their union produces the passions of hope and fear.

ON this subject, a very curious question may be raised concerning that conflict of passions which is our present topic. It is observable that where the objects of opposing passions are presented at once, besides the increase of the dominant passion (which has already been explained and usually arises at their first clash or encounter), it sometimes happens that both passions exist successively and at short intervals; sometimes, they destroy each other, and neither occurs; and sometimes, both remain united in the mind. WHEN the contrary passions arise from objects entirely different, they take place alternately; the want of relation in the ideas separating the impressions from each other, and preventing their opposition. Thus when a man is afflicted for the loss of a lawsuit, and joyful for the birth of a son, the mind running from the agreeable to the calamitous object, with whatever celerity it may perform this motion, can scarcely temper the one affection with the other, and remain between them in a state of indifference.

IT more easily attains that calm situation, when the same event is of a mixed nature, and contains something adverse and something prosperous in its different circumstances. For in that case, both the passions, mingling with each other by means of the relation, become mutually destructive, and leave the mind in perfect tranquility.

Contrary passions are not capable of destroying each other, except when their contrary movements exactly clash and are opposite in their direction, as well as in the sensation they produce. This exact clash depends upon the relations of those ideas from which they are derived, and is more or less perfect according to the degrees of the relation. In the case of probability, the contrary chances are so far related that they determine the existence or non-existence of the same object. But this relation is far from being perfect; since some of the chances lie on the side of existence, and others on that of non-existence, which are objects altogether incompatible. It is impossible by one steady view to survey the opposite chances and the events dependent on them; but it is necessary that the imagination should run alternately from the one to the other. Each view of the imagination produces its peculiar passion, which decays away by degrees, and is

It is in this manner that hope and fear arise from the different mixture of these opposite passions of grief and joy, and from their imperfect union and conjunction.

ON the whole, contrary passions succeed each other alternately when they arise from different objects: They mutually destroy each other when they proceed from different parts of the same object: And they both subsist and mingle together when they are derived from the contrary and incompatible chances or possibilities on which any one object depends. The influence of the relations of ideas is plainly seen in this whole matter. If the objects of the contrary passions are totally different, the passions are like two opposite liquids in different bottles, which have no influence on each other. If the objects are intimately connected, the passions are like an *alkali* and an *acid*, which, being mingled, destroy each other. If the relationship is more imperfect, and consists in the

Since the hypothesis concerning hope and fear carries its own evidence with it, we shall be more concise in our proofs. A few strong arguments are better than many weak ones.

THE passions of fear and hope may arise when the chances are equal on both sides, and no superiority can be discovered in one over the other. Indeed, in this situation, the passions are at their strongest, as the mind then has the least foundation to rest upon and is tossed with the greatest uncertainty. Add a higher degree of probability to the side of grief, and you immediately see that passion diffuse itself over the composition and tint it into fear. Increase the probability, and by that means the grief; the fear prevails more and more, until at last—as the joy continually diminishes—it transitions imperceptibly into pure grief. After you have brought it to this situation, diminish the grief in the same manner that you increased it: by diminishing the probability on that side, and

Are these not as plain proofs—that the passions of fear and hope are mixtures of grief and joy—as it is a proof in optics that a colored ray of the sun passing through a prism is a composition of two others, when, as you diminish or increase the quantity of either, you find it prevail proportionally more or less in the composition? I am sure neither natural nor moral philosophy admits of stronger proofs.

PROBABILITY is of two kinds: either when the object is really in itself uncertain and to be determined by chance; or when, though the object is already certain, it is yet uncertain to our judgment, which finds a number of proofs on each side of the question. Both these kinds of probabilities cause fear and hope; which can only proceed from that property in which they agree, namely, the uncertainty and fluctuation they give to the imagination by that opposition of views which is common to both.

It is a probable good or evil that commonly produces hope or fear; because probability, being a wavering and inconstant method of surveying an object, naturally causes a similar mixture and uncertainty of passion. But we may observe that wherever this mixture can be produced from other causes, the passions of fear and hope will arise, even though there is no probability; this must be allowed to be a convincing proof of the present hypothesis.

WE find that an evil, barely conceived as *possible*, sometimes produces fear, especially if the evil is very great. A man cannot think of excessive pains and tortures without trembling if he is in the least danger of suffering them. The smallness of the probability is compensated by the greatness of the evil; and the sensation is just as lively as if the evil were more probable. One view or glimpse of the former has the same effect as several of the latter.

EVILS that are *certain* sometimes have the same effect in producing fear as the possible or impossible. Thus, a man in a strong, well-guarded prison, without the slightest means of escape, trembles at the thought of the rack to which he is sentenced. This happens only when the certain evil is terrible and overwhelming; in which case the mind continually rejects it with horror, while it continually presses in upon the thought. The evil is fixed and established there, but the mind cannot endure to focus on it; from this fluctuation and uncertainty, there arises a passion of much the same appearance as fear.

From these principles, we may account for a phenomenon in the passions which at first sight seems very extraordinary: *namely*, that surprise is apt to change into fear, and everything that is unexpected frightens us. The most obvious conclusion from this is that human nature is in general pusillanimous <note>Lacking courage or resolution; cowardly.</note>; since upon the sudden appearance of any object we immediately conclude it to be an evil, and without waiting until we can examine its nature—whether it be good or bad—we are at first affected with fear. Upon further examination, we shall find that the phenomenon is to be accounted for in another way. The suddenness and strangeness of an appearance naturally excite a commotion in the mind, like everything for which we are not prepared and to which we are not accustomed. This commotion, in turn, naturally produces a curiosity or inquisitiveness; being very violent due to the strong and sudden impulse of the object, it becomes uneasy and resembles—in its fluctuation and uncertainty—the sensation of fear or the mixed passions of grief and joy. This image of fear naturally converts into the thing itself and gives us a real apprehension of evil, as the mind always forms its judgments more from its present disposition than from the nature of its objects.

THUS, all kinds of uncertainty have a strong connection with fear, even though they do not cause any opposition of passions through the opposing views and considerations they present to us. A person who has left a friend suffering from any illness will feel more anxiety on their account than if they were present, even though they might be not only incapable of providing assistance but also of judging the outcome of the sickness. In this case, though the principal object of the passion—namely, the life or death of his friend—is just as uncertain to him when present as when absent, there are still a thousand small circumstances of his friend's situation and condition, the knowledge of which stabilizes the idea and prevents that fluctuation and uncertainty so closely allied to fear. Uncertainty is, indeed, in one respect as closely allied to hope as to fear, since it forms an essential part of the composition of the former passion; but the reason why it does not incline to that side is that uncertainty itself is uneasy, and it shares a relationship of impressions with the uneasy passions.

It is thus that our uncertainty concerning any minor circumstance relating to a person increases our apprehensions of their death or misfortune. *Horace* has remarked upon this phenomenon.

<margin>PART III. — *Of the will and direct passions.*</margin>observe that any doubt produces that passion, even though it presents nothing to us on any side but what is good and desirable. A virgin, on her bridal night, goes to bed full of fears and apprehensions, though she expects nothing but pleasure of the highest kind, and what she has long wished for. The newness and greatness of the event, and the confusion of wishes and joys, so embarrass the mind that it knows not on what passion to fix itself; from this arises a fluttering or unsettledness of the spirits, which being, in some degree, uneasy, very naturally degenerates into fear.

Thus we still find that whatever causes any fluctuation or mixture of passions, with any degree of uneasiness, always produces fear, or at least a passion so like it that they are scarcely to be distinguished.

I have here confined myself to the examination of hope and fear in their most simple and natural situation, without considering all the variations they may receive from the mixture of different views and reflections. *Terror*, *consternation*, *astonishment*, *anxiety*, and other passions of that kind, are nothing but different species and degrees

It is easy to imagine how a different situation of the object, or a different turn of thought, may change even the sensation of a passion; and this may in general account for all the particular subdivisions of the other affections, as well as of fear. Love may show itself in the shape of *tenderness*, *friendship*, *intimacy*, *esteem*, *goodwill*, and in many other appearances; which at the bottom are the same affections, and arise from the same causes, though with a small variation, which it is not necessary to give any particular account of. It is for this reason I have all along confined myself to the principal passion.

THE same care of avoiding prolixity is the reason why I waive the examination of the will and direct passions, as they appear in animals; since nothing is more evident, than that they are of the same nature, and excited by the same causes as in human creatures. I leave this to the reader's own observation; desiring him at the same time to consider the additional force this bestows on the present system.

## SECT. X.

### *Of curiosity, or the love of truth.*

<margin>
SECT. X.

But it seems to me that we have been quite inattentive to have run over so many different parts of the human mind, and examined so many passions, without once taking into consideration that love of truth, which was the first source of all our inquiries. It will therefore be proper, before we leave this subject, to offer a few reflections on that passion, and show its origin in human nature. It is an affection of such a unique kind, that it would have been impossible to have treated it under any of those headings which we have examined without the danger of obscurity and confusion.

TRUTH is of two kinds, consisting either in the discovery of the proportions of ideas, considered as such, or in the conformity of our ideas of objects to their real existence. It is certain that the former type of truth is not desired merely as truth, and that it is not the

For these conclusions are equally accurate when we discover the equality of two bodies using a pair of compasses as when we learn it through a mathematical demonstration; and although in the one case the proofs are demonstrative, and in the other only based on the senses, yet generally speaking, the mind rests with equal assurance in the one as in the other. And in an arithmetical operation, where both the truth and the certainty are of the same nature as in the most profound algebraic problem, the pleasure is very minor, if it does not actually turn into pain. This is clear proof that the satisfaction which we sometimes receive from the discovery of truth proceeds not from it merely as such, but only when it is endowed with certain qualities.

THE first and most important condition required to make truth agreeable is the <term>genius</term> and <term>capacity</term> which is employed in its invention and discovery. What is easy and obvious is never valued; and even what is in *itself* difficult, if we come to the knowledge of it without diffi-

<margin>
PART III.
{
Of the will and direct passions.
</margin>

culty, and without any stretch of thought or judgment, is held in little regard. We love to trace the demonstrations of mathematicians, but we would receive little entertainment from a person who simply informed us of the proportions of lines and angles, even if we placed the utmost confidence in both his judgment and truthfulness. In this case, it is enough just to have ears to learn the truth. We are never required to focus our attention or exert our <term>genius</term>; which, of all exercises of the mind, is the most pleasant and agreeable.

**BUT** although the exercise of <term>genius</term> is the principal source of that satisfaction we receive from the sciences, I doubt whether it is alone sufficient to give us any significant enjoyment. The truth we discover must also be of some importance. It is easy to multiply algebraic problems to infinity, and there is no end to discovering the proportions of conic sections; yet few mathematicians take any pleasure in these researches, but instead turn their thoughts to what is more useful and important. Now the question is: in what manner do this utility and importance affect us?

<summary>Hume argues that while the intellectual effort of "genius" is the main source of pleasure in discovery, it is not enough on its own; the truth discovered must also be perceived as useful or important. He notes that mathematicians generally ignore trivial problems in favor of those that hold greater significance or utility.</summary>

SECT. X.

us? The difficulty on this point arises from the fact that many philosophers have consumed their time, have destroyed their health, and neglected their fortune in the search for such truths as they considered important and useful to the world, though it appeared from their whole conduct and behavior that they were not endowed with any share of public spirit, nor had any concern for the interests of mankind. If they were convinced that their discoveries were of no consequence, they would entirely lose all enthusiasm for their studies, even though the consequences are entirely indifferent to them; this seems to be a contradiction.

To resolve this contradiction, we must consider that there are certain desires and inclinations which go no further than the imagination, and are rather the faint shadows and images of passions than any real affections. Thus, suppose a man who surveys the fortifications of any city; considers their strength and advantages, whether natural or acquired; observes the layout and design of the bastions, ramparts, mines, and other military works; it is plain that, in proportion as all these are suited to achieve their goals, he will receive a suitable

This pleasure, as it arises from the utility, not the form of the objects, can be nothing other than a sympathy with the inhabitants, for whose security all this art is employed; though it is possible that this person, as a stranger or an enemy, may in his heart have no kindness for them, or may even entertain a hatred against them.

It may indeed be objected that such a remote sympathy is a very slight foundation for a passion, and that so much industry and application, as we frequently observe in philosophers, can never be derived from such an inconsiderable origin. But here I return to what I have already remarked: that the pleasure of study consists chiefly in the action of the mind, and the exercise of genius and understanding in the discovery or comprehension of any truth. If the importance of the truth is required to complete the pleasure, it is not on account of any considerable addition which it brings to our enjoyment by itself, but only because it is, in some measure, required to fix our attention. *Of curio-*

</margin>
But besides the action of the mind, which is the principal foundation of the pleasure, there is likewise required a degree of success in the attainment of the end, or the discovery of that truth we examine. Upon this head I shall make a general remark, which may be useful on many occasions, *namely*, that where the mind pursues any end with passion; though that passion be not derived originally from the end, but merely from the action and pursuit; yet by the natural course of the affections, we acquire a concern for the end itself, and are uneasy under any disappointment we meet with in the pursuit of it. This proceeds from the relation and parallel direction of the passions mentioned above.

To illustrate all this by a similar instance, I shall observe that there cannot be two passions more nearly resembling each other than those of hunting and philosophy, whatever disproportion may at first sight appear between them. It is evident that the pleasure of hunting consists in the action of the mind and body; the motion, the attention,

<summary>Hume argues that even if the pleasure of a pursuit comes from the mental activity itself, the mind naturally develops a genuine concern for reaching the final goal. He begins to illustrate this by comparing the passions of philosophy and hunting.</summary>

<meta>This page continues David Hume’s comparison between the pursuit of truth in philosophy and the pursuit of game in hunting. It elaborates on how the perceived utility of a goal, even if minor, is necessary to sustain the mind's interest during the activity.</meta>

<page-num>314</page-num>

PART III.
---
*Of the will and direct passions.*
</margin>

the difficulty, and the uncertainty. It is likewise evident that these actions must be accompanied by an idea of utility in order for them to have any effect upon us. A man of the greatest fortune, and the farthest removed from avarice, though he takes pleasure in hunting partridges and pheasants, feels no satisfaction in shooting crows and magpies; and that is because he considers the first as fit for the table, and the other as entirely useless. Here it is certain that the utility or importance does not cause any real passion by itself, but is only required to support the imagination; and the same person, who overlooks a ten times greater profit in any other subject, is pleased to bring home half a dozen woodcocks or plovers after having employed several hours in hunting for them. To make the parallel between hunting and philosophy more complete, we may observe that although in both cases the goal of our action may be despised in itself, yet in the heat of the action we acquire such an attention to this goal that we are very uneasy under any disappointments, and are sorry when we either miss our game or fall into any error in our reasoning.

<meta>Catchword: If</meta>

<summary>Hume explains that while the pleasure of a pursuit like hunting or philosophy comes from the activity itself, a sense of utility is necessary to fix the mind's attention on the goal. He notes that once engaged in the "heat of the action," the hunter or philosopher becomes genuinely concerned with achieving the end result, however trivial it might otherwise seem.</summary>

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>

<margin>
Sect. X.
Of curiosity, or the love of truth.
</margin>

If we want another parallel to these affections, we may consider the passion of gaming, which affords a pleasure from the same principles as hunting and philosophy. It has been remarked that the pleasure of gaming arises not from interest alone, since many leave a sure gain for this entertainment; neither is it derived from the game alone, since the same persons have no satisfaction when they play for nothing. Instead, it proceeds from both these causes united, though separately they have no effect. It is here as in certain chemical preparations, where the mixture of two clear and transparent liquids produces a third, which is opaque and colored.

The interest which we have in any game engages our attention, without which we can have no enjoyment, either in that or in any other action. Our attention being once engaged, the difficulty, variety, and sudden reverses of fortune interest us still further; and it is from that concern our satisfaction arises. Human life is so tiresome a scene, and men generally are of such indolent dispositions, that whatever amuses them, though by a passion mixed with pain, does in the main give them a sensible pleasure. And

<meta>catchword: this</meta>
<vocab>passions, gaming, philosophy, curiosity, love of truth, chemical preparations, human life</vocab>

<summary>Hume compares the pursuit of truth and hunting to the passion of gaming, noting that the pleasure arises only when a personal interest in the outcome is combined with the challenges of the activity itself. He argues that humans are naturally prone to boredom, making any engaging activity—even one involving stress or pain—preferable to inactivity.</summary>
<keywords>David Hume, gaming, gambling, psychology of pleasure, curiosity, interest, human nature, chemical analogy</keywords>

this pleasure is increased here by the nature of the objects, which—being perceptible and having a narrow scope—are engaged with easily and are agreeable to the imagination.

The same theory that explains the love of truth in mathematics and algebra may be extended to morals, politics, <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>natural science</note>, and other studies where we consider not the abstract relations of ideas, but their real connections and existence. But besides the love of knowledge, which displays itself in the sciences, there is a certain curiosity implanted in human nature, which is a passion derived from a completely different principle. Some people have an insatiable desire to know the actions and circumstances of their neighbors, even though their own interests are in no way involved, and they must depend entirely on others for their information; in such cases, there is no room for study or application. Let us search for the reason for this phenomenon.

It has been proven at length that the influence of belief is simultaneously to enliven and fix any idea in the imagination, and to prevent all kinds of hesitation and uncertainty about it. Both these circumstances are ad-

<meta>This page continues David Hume’s discussion on the psychological foundations of curiosity. Having established that belief enlivens and stabilizes ideas, he now explores how the mind’s aversion to doubt and sudden change drives the desire for certainty and the "love of truth."</meta>

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header>Book II. Of the Passions.</header>

vantageous. <margin>SECTION X. *Of curiosity, or the love of truth.*</margin> By the intensity of the idea, we engage the imagination and produce—though to a lesser degree—the same pleasure that arises from a moderate passion. Just as the intensity of the idea gives pleasure, its certainty prevents uneasiness by fixing one particular idea in the mind and keeping it from wavering in the choice of its objects. It is a quality of human nature, conspicuous on many occasions and common to both the mind and body, that a change that is too sudden and violent is unpleasant to us; and however indifferent any objects may be in themselves, their alteration causes uneasiness. Since it is the nature of doubt to cause a variation in thought and to transport us suddenly from one idea to another, it must consequently be a source of pain. This pain mainly occurs where interest, a personal connection, or the greatness and novelty of an event engages us in it. We do not have a curiosity to be informed of every fact, nor are we curious only about those facts we have a personal interest in knowing. It is enough if the idea strikes us with such force and concerns us so closely that its instability and inconstancy cause us uneasiness. A stranger,

<meta>catchword: when</meta>

<summary>Hume argues that curiosity is driven by the mind's natural aversion to the pain of doubt and sudden fluctuations in thought. He explains that we seek certainty because a vivid or significant idea causes uneasiness when it remains unstable or unsettled in our imagination.</summary>
<keywords>curiosity, love of truth, imagination, doubt, certainty, human nature, instability, passion, psychology of belief</keywords>

XXXII. The Doctrine of Hell-Torments distinctly and impartially discussed. price 1 s. The last three by one Author.

XXXIII. An Inquiry into the Law of Nature and Revelation. To which is annexed, A Discourse concerning the Mediator between God and Man. price 1 s. 6 d.

XXXIV. The Argument *à priori* concerning the Existence and Perfections of God, and its Importance to Virtue and True Religion, stated and considered. Together with the Difficulties and Objections which have anywhere occurred; particularly in a Dissertation, by a learned hand, at the end of Mr. *Law*'s Treatise of Space, etc. price 1 s.

XXXV. A Discourse concerning Virtue and Religion: Occasioned by some recent Writings. price 6 d. The last two written by one Author.

XXXVI. Divine Benevolence: or an Attempt to prove that the principal End of Divine Providence and Government is the Happiness of his Creatures. Being an Answer to a Pamphlet, entitled, Divine Rectitude; or an Inquiry concerning the Moral Perfections of the Deity. With the Refutation of the notions therein advanced concerning Beauty and Order, the Reason of Punishment, and the Necessity of a State of Trial antecedent to perfect Happiness. price 1 s.

XXXVII. An Introduction to the Doctrine of <term>Fluxions</term>, and Defense of the Mathematicians against the Objections of the Author of the *Analyst*, so far as they are designed to affect their general Methods of Reasoning. price 1 s. These last two by the same Author.

XXXVIII. An Essay in Defense of the Use and Advantage of Prayer. Wherein Objections raised from the Attributes and Decrees of God, and the settled Course and Order of Things, are considered and answered. By the late Rev. Mr. *John Archer*. price 6 d.

XXXIX. A Question proposed by the Author of an Address to the Inhabitants of London and Westminster, namely, Whether God is an arbitrary Being and commands only for Commanding's sake, such Things as only oblige by Virtue of the Command, and can oblige no longer, considered. price 6 d.

XL. A Discourse of Natural and Revealed Religion, and the Relation they bear to each other. By the late Right Hon. the Lord Viscount *Barrington*. price 6 d.

XLI. An Argument to prove the Unity and Perfections of God *à priori*. price 6 d.

Price 3 d. The last ten pieces were written by a country clergyman.
